The one at Helmsdals in Sutherlandshire owes its-existence to the Duke anti Duchess of Sutherland, and the Duke and Duchess of Portland, the object being to provide work for the women and girls who have few other sources of employment when the herring fishery A New Liability for Hquseholdere.—The probability of the extensiem of the Workmen's Compensation Act, to domestic servants is a matter that concerns every householder who keeps a staff of servants, or even a single-handed maid. The cost of insurance against snch liability would not be heavy, lxit it would mean that this obligation would devolve upon every employer of domestic labour. It is to be feared too, thst it will open the door to a new field for exploitation to domestic malingerers. There are numbers of servants who go from one situation to another never intending to do any iwork, who will no doubt find it easy to cultivate ilight accidents, for which they will make a claim for compensation. That will mean an additional cause of trouble when theyare sent rbhout their business, with the month's wages they have not earned. About Underskirts.—There is a tendency this summer whenever transparent fabrics are employed for gowne,to avoid silky effects, and when a silk slip is worn it is of the softest silk procurable, and supplemented by an overdress of crepe de chine or eilk muslin. The up-to- date woman however avoids silk underskb ts as far as she possibly can, and would probably need it soothing draught if anyone was to suggest a silk underdress for wear with a lingerie gown, a mode which found great favour in our eyes a few seasons hack. Soft trailing effects are correct just now, and the woman who rustles is regarded with horror.

HINTS FOR THE SALES.

The sales are especially alluring this year, says the Lady, on account of the great reductions made on those items which are only suitable for warm weather. The cold snap during the end of May and the earlier days of June caused great loss to the big drapery houses, and an accumula- tion of summer things which must necessarily be cleared before the winter stock arrives. Ostrich boas, ruffles, and stoles /are so reduced 1; in price that many will be. rchased for the country or seaside holiday, an next spring the chemical cleaner will restore heir freshness and beauty for a few shillings. Parasols are. invari- ably cheap at the July sales, as any left on hand must be kept nine or tbn months. Lovely chiffon and lace sunshades are. reduced quite to• half- price. or even less.'

Deirable acquisitions for the• smart worpin are' oddments of lace of the type usually de- scribed as "made up." Of these the lace bolero is useful and adaptable, and not the least of its charms is that it serves either for day or even- ing wear. The bolero must be lined with chiffon, arid a plisse - frill or a bottillonnee of velvet. chiffon, or silk should outline the edge. So one should decide upon /the colour, and buy this also at the sales. Three to four yards of chiffon will line and outline a bolero with full plisse frills, or four to five yards will make a frilled •cover for a parasol.

Gloves of the washable type are useful for holi- day wear, especially the long lace •ar net kinds, or the lisle suede, which • pass for - suede when they f t closely. A, smaller size must be selected. such as 51 for the wearer of No. 6 in kid, and the twenty-button length is the most useful to wear with blouses or bodices, which are now generally made with elbow 'sleeves. The pale tan and lace shades are useful with all light cos- tumes, but brown and dark blue are admirable with these darker gowns fon holiday wear. Underwear, especially the hand-made kind, is sold very much under the regular. price during the sales, and this is done by 'the rrianufacturera to'keep workers busy in the slack season. If the cost of the various items' in a garment is calcu- lated at ordinary shop prices, it will be found that in the majority of cases the total works out beyond the sale price. without allowing one penny for make.

FANCY-WORX TR/FLES. ,We have before our 'notice a group of trifles that would _all be useful and ornamental addi- tions to a girl's wardrobe, and are so dainty that they would serve as pieces of fancy-work

. . for holiday times. The first is a yoke composed of -strips of insertion, having, 'the pattern -.accen- tv.ated by outlines of coloured silk, these strips being joined by a lattice. of babe ribbon match- ing the colours used in the lace. Secondly, we have in evening camisole in silk, made to slip over the. head, and trimmecb.with a band. edging, and motifs •of crochet•work in " ososilky " or some similar substitute for silk. The collar and panel front are fashioned of home-made needle- point lace, done with floniein braid, and edged with tiny frills of knife-pleated silk; and, lastly. the little turn-down linen collar is embroidered in silk, and has insets of drawn-thread work by way of trimming.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

SPINACH SALAD.-Wmh and pick well 21b. spinaCh. Place it in a saucepan and cook till tender. Then pas twice through a sieve, re- turn to the saucepan, and flavour with lemon juice, pepper, a little cayenne, and salt. Have ready some small moulds, decorate the bottom of half the number with a thin round of beetroot and others with finely-chopped white of egg. Put a spoonful of spinach when cold into each, ip.r.v- ing a deep impression in the cent:e. Fill this with a spoonful of good mayonrse sauce into which a little dissolved ••elatine has been stirred. placing halt the yolk of a hard-boiled egg tri addirkin in the 'middle of each mould. Place in the refrigerator to keep cool, and when ready for serving turn out the moulds on' to a bed of lettuce.

GROWTH OF IRON.

It will be news to most people that iron grows, but Mr. William Marriott, M.1.C.E., writing to the Times; says: With regard to the 'growth of iron,' I have never seen this in print before, nor have heard the term. used, but during my experience of over tiiirty years I have felt that such has been the case. Rails that have fitted swing briiiges with plenty, of clearance have had to be shortened repeatedly year after year, and only recently I have known an instance of a swing bridge, which had been open for half an hour, that could not be put back until some of the ironwork had been reduced. The bridge bad been built for some thirteen years, and had been opened and closed during that time many hun- dreds of times. There is little doubt in my mind that iron heated and cooled alternately does permanently lengthen." .

REV. DR. MERCER'S rKESIGNATION. A letter was read from the Local Government Board acknowledging the 'receipt of the Clerk's letter of the 21st ult., with reference to the resignation of the Rev. Dr. Mercer of the office of Guardian for St. John's Ward, and pointing out that his resignation had to be, tendered to them. The CLERK said Dr. Mercer bad been informed of the Local Government Board's requirement. TIE LATE MRS. S. DAVIES. The CLERK read the following letter from Mr. S. Davies,a member of the Board :—" Dear Mr. Sutton. It was with intense feeling that I received your letter con- veying to me the kind note of condolence of the Board in the sad loss which I have sustained in the death of my dear wife. Although it has been a severe blow to me, for the time being almost incapacitating me from following my work, such kind public expressions as these coming from esteemed colleagues, working together week by week for the good of those around us, do greatly help one to bear the great loss. I do indeed thank them. Especially do I thank you personally for your kind expression of sympathy which I very much appreciate. Yours very faithfully, S. Davits."

PALE, WEAK, WASTED.

What a Young Married Woman endured through Indigestion, Anmmia, Wind and Sleeplessness,

The terrible suffering which anemia inflicts upon thousands of people and particularly upon young women, is beyond estimating. You see its victims every day, in street, in factory and in shop—pale, bloodless faces, pallid lips, sunken eyes. The leltst effort tires them: and they drag through the day's work, longing for night and rest. And yet all this could be so easily prevented. Anomie is due to bad digestion and assimi- lation of food. If your • food does not digest thoroughly it fails to carry nourishment into your system for the making of healthy red blood, and so the body is starved. Read this story from Mrs. Chipper- field, a young married woman living at 84, Russell-st., Dereham-road, Norwich. It is dated May 3rd, 1906 : "Some time ago," she says,'• I began to saffer with indigestion. Food disagreed with me and there was always a tight pressure across my chest. I lost all taste for food, and when I did eat there was always pain at my stomach afterwards. I had dizzy attacks quite frequently, and I wasted away to a mere shadow. SIX MONTHS Of MISERY.

"I was very pale and weak and though I tried many things I found no relief. My doctor said I was suffer- ing from anmmia and indigestion but in six months' attendance he did not help me. At last I began to take Mother Seigel's Syrup. Its effect was surprising. The pains and wind quicklyleft me and I found my'strength returning. Soon I was back at my work again. lam now in splendid health and have never suffered in the least from that time."

This is an every day example of what Mother Seigel's Syrup can do It is an absolute cure not only for anzemla but for Indigestion in every possible form. Pain. wind, headache, constipation, skin and kidney troubles, all yield to its influence.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Local Government Board and Dr. Braide.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, under the , presidency of Mr. J. Billinge. There were also present: —Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Dr. Murray, Messrs. W. Hickman (vice-chairman), A. J. Jolley, S. Davies J. Payton, R. Bolton, T. H. Ashcroft, G. Thompson, A;Brooks, W. Houghton, G. Mather, R. Forshaw, W. Flannagan, J. Cook, M. W. Seton, T. Ritchie, C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master).

THE CHILDREN.

PROPOSAL TO SEND ROMAN _CATHOLICS TO A HOME.

_ MR. ASHCROFT'S PROTEST. At a meeting of the House Committee, Mr. Shearer submitted correspondence from the War Office relative to certain repayments which that authority required to be refunded as the amounts had been in excess of what they should have been, and he was instructed to reply that the Guardians had no power to refund the amounts in question. Owing to the unusually high number of children in the House, and the fact that there was not a single vacancy for girls in the Cottage Homes, it was resolved to recommend the Board to instruct the Clerk to ascertain from Catholic Homes upon what terms they were pre- pared to take twenty Catholic girls from Padgate. , Mr. FORSHAW moved the adoption of the minutes. The CLERK said the Runcorn Guardians had taken a great deal of trouble in making inquiries from different institutions with regard to the reception of Roman , Catholic children. He had written to the clerk of the i Board (Mr. G. P. Ashton) and received a reply stating ' that the Runcorn Guardians had sent Roman Catholic girls to the Pantasaph Orphanage for ten or twelve years and were very pleased indeed with the manner in which they were treated and turned out for service at the end of their training. The Board only paid 4s. per week for the maintenance of each child but he did not think the Home would accept new cases under ss. a week. Mr. Sutton suggested that two Guardians should visit Pantasaph for the purpose of ascertaining whether it was a suitable institution and what the terms were. Mr. MATHER thought the course suggested was satis- factory but he proposed that the whole question should first be referred to the Padgate Schools Committee. Hr. ASHCROFT seconded. He did not think the Clerk should have written about the Homes before the matter had been discussed by the committee. They bad no need whatever to send the children away. He had no doubt that with the assistance of the Superintendent and Mr. Shearer they could reduce the number of scholars in the Padgate schools. There were some children at Padgate over fourteen years of age who had no right to be in the schools and who ought to be at work. If a time came when the schools were over- crowded and the boys could not find work he would be prepared to send some to Homes, but not at present. Only recently an application for permission to remove a child over 14 years of age from the schools was ref used.

Mr. MATHER : I would not like it to go forth to the public that there are children in the Padgate schools who have no right to be there.

Mr. Asncnoi:r : I can prove that there are children in the place who have no right to be there. We can clear them out and then there will be no need to send any to Homes. lOM give the names if you choose. Mr. Fonsn.s.w said he believed there were about sixty children in the House and two thirds Were girls. They were not going to send one child away if they could possibly avoid it. Mr. MATHER : How many children are there over five years of age? Mr. AWIIRRY : About twenty-two. Mr. FORSHAW thought Mr. Ashcroft ought to thrash these matters out at the Padget° Committee meetings. Mr. ASHCROFT : We have not had a chance in this case. Mr. FORSHA.W ; You seem very fond of villifying these committees and their work. Mr. AhHcnorr : I will answer Mr. Forshaw (cries of " Order.") Mr. MATHER said the children whom it was con- sidered fitting to send away would be carefully picked out. _

The CHAIRMAN said that if the House Committee's minutes were approved the matter would eventually revert to the Padg,ate Committee. In the meantime several institutions could be visited. Mr. MATE! ER said he would withdraw his amendment. Mr. ASHCROFT would not consent to the withdrawal. On a vote being taken the amendruant was defeated and the minutes were approved._ _

It was then proposed that Mr. Mather and Mrs. Clay should visit Puntasaph Orphansgd and several other institutions' Mr. ASHCROFT : I oppose it. It is a waste of public money. There is no need for the children to go away. The proposition was agreed to. The CHAIRMAN: Where .are you going to send them ?—to Poplar? (Laughter).

Two men were ushered into the private aloe of Abraham Levy, the lawyer, the other day and stood in silence before him. "Well," said Mr. Levy. "You ask him," said one of the men in a hoarse whisper. "Wait a minute," said the other, "maybe he'll guess it." "Come; come, gentlemen, my time is valuable," interrupted Mr. Levy. "We are twin brothers," chorused the two, and we thought you'd have guessed it." Mr. Levy looked anxiously at the door, at the same time glancing fur- tively at the two men, and continued in a faint voice; "Is that all ?" "No,' continued one of the strange pair, "we wish to ask you a question. Our father died a short time ago. We were his only el- ildren. He left a paper in which he said that his oldest son was to have all his property, but neither of us is the oldest, so what are we going to do ?" "How much did your father leave ?" asked Mr. Levy. "Seven dollars," ehorussed the two men. "Divide it," answered the lawyer. "But what is your fee for this advice ?' asked one of the men. "Seven dollars," replied Mr. Levy. The two men produced the fee and paid it over, saying at the same time that Mr. Levy liad•relieved them of a great mental burden. Then they left the office beaming. Boy: "He noticed that you'd got a new one, and he wants to borrow that. This one's most worn A YeftG man, whose features and flashing eyes betokened great earnestness, was summoned before Judge McCarthy of the Washington City Court the other day for jury duty. He immediately asked to be excused. Wfien the judge asked him what excuse he had for not serving, he replied: "I believe it is a rule of the court that the jury is the solo judge of the facts and the court of the law—that the juror should only weigh the facts as presented -by the evidence, not taking into con- sideration any of the rules of law governing the case; wherefore all lawyers are exempt from jury duty." " But are you a lawyer ?" asked Judge McCarthy. "No, but I have been a close student of the law for many years." "I am afraid that I cannot excuse you if you are not a lawyer," said the court, smiling., "But," continued the young man, with great earnestness, the colour mounting to his temples, "I am sure if your honour knew as much law as I do, your conscience would not allow you to serve on a jury." After the bench and bar had recovered, from this naive outburst the judge told the young man that if it was a matter which affected his conscience 50 deeply he would excuse him, and a very much aliashed youth left the court- room.

PRILANTHROPY, that darling of his heart, lately played a pretty trick on Andrew Carnegie. A few mornings ago he and another gentleman—lds sec- retary, presumably—left the Carnegie house, in Fifty-fourth-street, and stepped toward a carriage standing at the curb. They had moved but a few paces when, coming from nowhere in particular, a wretched, ragged woman interrupted their advance, "God bless you, gentlemen," she said, holding forth her hand. Her voice was irresistibly pathetic. Unbuttoning his coat, Mr. Carnegie drew a coin from his WlLlStcoat pocket, and without glancing at it, handed it to tlie• woman.. The car- riage door Was opened, and Mr. Carnegie places one foot on the step. Then be drew ba6k---in deep thought, apparently. He turned quickly to the woman. " Here, My good woman," he said; fumbling in another pocket, " let Me hake the money I just gave you." For a moment the woman re- garded him with eyes anything but pathetic. Then. opening her band, she glanced at the coin which he had given to her. "Oh! It's a keepsake, is it ?" she exclaimed, boldly. " Yes. It nothing to you." "It's nothing to me, but it everything to you. What'll you give me for it ?" Mr. Carnegie surrendered to his undignified predicament. "Very well," he answered, though visibly annoyed. So; handhig the woman a bill, he received the keep- sake in exchange. " she's a slick one that," said Un versity Club "cabby.' who had aserved the incident. RAPID ORE HANDLING.

According to Cassier's Magazine. during the past ten years no • less than 140,000,000 tons of iron ore have been taken from the ranges adja- Tent to Lake Superior and transported to the furnaces in the Pittsburg district, the Ohio Valley, and elsewheic. These figures are of such magnitude that the extent of the industry can scarcely be appreciated. It is due entirely to the remarkable development which has been attained in machinery for excavating the ore, unloading the ore carriers, and for serving the furnaces, as by using mechanical unloaders and conveyors the cost of transporting a ton of iron ore from Duluth, for example, to Conneaut has been re- duced from 3dol. to 60 cents. In speaking of the increased size of the cargoes of ore now carried from Lake Superior and Lake Michigan, the article remarks that , the rapidity with which ships' holds arc filled is almost marvellous. ,At any one set of docks St Duluth a fleet of fourteen vessels has been filled with 64,000. tons or ore in twenty-four hours, while the steamer Wolvin, one of the largest carrieNs, has taken on 12,250 tons in the actual loading time of one hour and thirty minutes, being at the dock only three hours hi all.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
POISONOUS- WALLPAPERS.

Almost all green wallpaper is said to be poi- sonous, as arsenic is used. to produce that colour. Some red papers have also a certain amount of arsenic. According to the Family Doctor, it is wise to ascertain before paper is put on the wall whether, it contains a poisonous ingredient. This ca .i be done in the following manner: Soak a piece of the paper in a. concentrated solution of sodium nitrate and equal parts of water and alcohol; that is, add the sodium until the liquid dissolves no more of it. Burn the paper in a saucer—it smoulders rather than flames. Pour water over the ashes, boil, and filter. The filtrate is acidified with dilute sulphuric acid, and per- manganate of potash is added slowly as long as th?. red colour disappears or changes to yellow- brown upon warming, and finally a slight excess of chameleon solution is present. If the liquid becomes turbid, it is to be filtered. After cool- ing more dilute sulphuric acid is added, and also a piece of pure clean zinc, and the flask closed with a cork split in two places. In one split of the cork a piece of paper moistened in silver nitrate is fastened, in the other a strip of parch- ment paper dipped in sugar of lead. If arsenic is present the silver soon blackens. The lead paper is merely a check on presence of sulphuric acid. ,

CUCKOOS AND THEIR EGGS.

Mr. Oswald H. Latter, Of Charterhouse, has an interesting study on the question of cuckoo's eggs in Bionzetrika. Are the eggs usually coloured like those in the nest where they are placed? And if so, what is the reason and ex- planation? It is concluded that in a large num- ber of cases there is a certain agreement of colour and marking. As regards the explanation as to how this has conic about, Mr. Latter him- self inclines to the view put forward by Pro- fessor Newton. that the explanation is on the lines of natural selection. Thus, a cuckoo favour- ing a titlark's nest laid an egg marked like that of the foster-parent. The latter being deceived, this cuckoo succeeded better in leaving offspring than those which laid eggs differing from the foster-parents. The offspring also laid eggs marked like the titlark's, and placed them in tit- larks' nests. Thus, what may be called a race of titlark-cuckoos might be formed. In a similar way races of wagtail-cuckoos, robin-cuckoos, &c., might be evolved. There are, however, ,some serious difficulties iii,the way of accepting this hypothesis, declares the Globe. In the first place tho number of cases in which the resemblance is at all close does not amount to one-half of the total cases: The hedge- sparrow is a frequent foster-parent, and yet no hlue cuckoo's egg has ever been found in this country. Nor has it be shewn that a larger number of young cuckoos are reared from those eggs in which the resemblance is perfect than from those where there is no resemblance. Again, there is no Rviderwo that birds take any particular notice of the colour and markings of the eggs on which they sit. Many cases, indeed, are known which seem to indicate that they are as a rules indifferent. And if the foster-parents did really carefully scrutinise their egg; not even the moat perfectly imitative cuckoo's egg would deceive them: they would surely know each individual egg they had laid. The ref.em- blame, be it noted, is never sufficient.to deceive the ornithologist. The itiference, indeed, seems irresistible that birds are either easily deceive& in the matter of eggs, or else do not mind the imposition of the alien egg.

HIGH PRESSURE -WATER POWER.

Hydro-Electric 'Company has erected a plant at Manitou, near Colorado Springs, in which : water wheeli of the Pelton type are operated under the enormous 'head of 2,4171 t., which is, with one exception, the highest yet utilised in soy hydraulic power installation in the world. The use of water for power at the great pressure due to such a head involves the solution of special problems of exceptional character,. but these have been courageously attacked and solved by the engineers of this plant. The water is col- lected high up on the mountain side, and do-' scends through a. pipe to the generating station in the valley from whence, after turning the wheels, it is 'discharged into the town mains, thus being made to serve a double useful purpose. The entire length of the pipe leading from the source of supply to the station is 4,7751 t.; part of which consists of a light pipe on a slight gradient, an the rest of the pressure pipe, which is a heavy pipe descending the hillside and laid in a trough—filled in with concrete, and of 21in. internal diameter. Deducting the loss of head due to friction in the pipe. the. effective head act- ing upon the wheels is 2,200 ft., and the pressure to which the pipe is subjected may therefore rise to about I,ooolb. per square inch, to resist which is made of sheet steel, with seams of .lotible-butt straps. Great trouble was experi- enced in making the joints of the pipe, as tests chewed that the lead was squeezed out in a thin film by a pressure such as the pipe would have to withstand in service. An alloy of tin and lead for the gaskets was found to be satisfactory for the .purpose.

h •T" e water issues from small nozzles at a velocity of more than 300 ft. per• second (204 miles per hour), and strikes the wheel buckets by which part of the energy in the stream is transferred to the dynamos. As it would be impossible to govern such a stream by throttling, owing to the retardation of the mass of water which would wreck the pipe, the variation in load is met by deflecting part of the stream past the buckets by a movement a( the nozzles. As the impact of tll:s stream would quickly wear through hard sub- stances, the momentum is destroyed by directing it into a pool, the tAil race 'having been so designed as to maintain a pool 40ft. long through which the fierce jet expends energy, and ter- minates upon a past iron baffle plate at the end. The danger referred to of throttling a jet suffi- ciently fast, to be of use as a governing device inay be inferred from the fact that the closing valves, to which the attendant has access, are so erranged that they cannot ba .4.,ased in less than r went v-nve minutes, toe operation tieing oar- forme'd through worm and wheel gearing by an electric motor. Even at this slow rate of retaraa- tion of the water the pressure developed in the pipe is by no means inconsiderable.

THE FUTURE OF LABOUR.

Important Amalgamation Pronosal.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held at the Bold-street schools on Tuesdsy evening. Mr. Plumb preside& over a small attendance.

Councillor STorr who for two years has occupied the position of vice-president of the Lancashire and Ches- hire federation Of Trades Councils, was re-elected the Council's delegate to the Federation. Mr, GREALY in moving his re-appointment said he thought it only fair that Councillor Stott should be elected in order that he might have an opportunity of becoming the president of the federatibtk.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
-1:i-BOSTR R 611 ES ENT TION COMMITTEE'S REPORt.

Mr. CORBIN, delegate to the Labour Representation Committee reported that at the last meeting of the Committee the greater portion of the time was taken up in organizing in preparation for the coming November elections.A motion to change the name of the committee from Labour Representation Committee to La hour Party was defeated. The committee recommended that a demonstration be held in summer at a costnot to exceed 94. Mr. PLUMB asked what kind of a demonstration could be held for .£4. Mr. CORBIN explained that the proceedings would be held in the open air. Mr. PLUMB was appointed delegate to the Labour Representation Committee in the place of Mr. Higgin- bottom who retired.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
_ PROPOSED AMALGAMATION OF LABOUR .BODIES.

.4:',A.a7Mejiiini- of the Executive Committee of the Council it was resolved "That in the opinion of this Committee the interest of local trades unions would be better served by one organization to deal with all trade unions—political and general—and invite the Labour' Representation Committee and Right to Work Com- mittee to apoint delegates to meet representatives from the Trades Council and discuss the question at an early date." _ . .

Mr. PLumß in moving the adoption ,of the report said the committee nnanimonsly felt that the time had come when they should make an attempt to unify the trades unions of the town. At the present time, they were split up into the Trades Council, Labour Representation Committee and the Right to Work Committee and there was a good deal of overlapping and moreover their funds, little as they were, were being frittered away and nothing was being done. Mr. EDGE seconded. _ _ I

Mr. HIGGINBOTTOM supported the recommendation which be thought was a step in the right direction. When all was said and done the Trades Council ought to be the representative body and instead of being as they were now, they might be a body to be feared. Mr. PLuxtri said there were many things to be con- sidered but in the meantime time and money were being wasted and both were precious. Mr. RANDLES strongly supported the recommendation as it would give them the advantage of being able to fight, and net sit still as they were doing. The old trade unionist was now dead. The work to be done was political and they must be up and doing. They had been robbed and would continue to be robbed unless they secured direct labour representation. Mr. CORRIN said if it were not for the local press there would be no Trades Council at all, and if it were not for the press they would do nothing. It was sug- gested that the Trades Council, Labour Representation Committee and Right to Work Committee should cease to exist, and that a new body should spring up and take the place of the three. Mr. PLINSTON, in supporting the motion, said it was felt by the Executive Committee that by a federation of the three bodies into one they would be able to do a great amount of good work- He did not admit that had it not been for the press the Trades Council would have been dead in the town. They had done a good work, but not as much as they ought to have done, owing to their old-fashioned rules. The Labour Representa- tion Committee had not done the work they ought to have done. The party in Warringion which had done useful work was the Independent Labour party, which had looked after the interests of the ratepayers by send- ing three representatives to the Town Council. Councillor STorr said it seemed to him that both the Liberal and Conservative parties had become afraid of fighting elections and therefore the Labour party would have to take a prominent part in Municipal elections. He said that no party should be allowed to return two men in a short time without opposition and it did not speak well far the great political parties that they would allow an organization to return two men at so little expense. When the Labour Representation Com- mittee was formed he was hopeful that they would be able to bring out a strong labour organization in War- rington but unfortunately that had not been done. In twelve months' time instead of being built up the membership of the Labour Representation Committee had gone down. They were in the position that the Trades Council could not afford to contest municipal elections. The Labour Representation Committee was not sufficiently large to be able to contest elections out of the funds and the only thing they could show as the result of their work was the return of Councillor Wright and his election could not be really regarded as a trial of strength with either of the political parties, so that practically the Labour Representation Com- mittee had never been successful in returning a man to , the Council with the strength of a political party against them. Instead of being built up the Labour Representation Council had becorae gradually weaker and weaker and not only had the Labour Repre- sentation Committee gone down but the Trades Council had also been crippled. He wanted to see one central body of purely trade unions. He was confident if they brought abont the proposed federation they would be much better off financially,and moreover societies which at present hesitated whether to join the Trades Council or the Labour Representation Committee would at once join the combined organiza- tion. Unless the federation was brought about the Labour Representation Committee and the Trades Council would both dwindle in membership and not until they got centralization would they be able to I wield the power in Warrington which they as a body of intelligent men ought to hold. Labour was feared but it was only organized labour which was feared and if they in Warrington remained disorganized as they I were now they would not be feared. Mr. MOORFTELD strongly supported the proposal because he felt that if the forces of labour were not brought forward they would not have the same effect. Mr. PODSIOEE said that like the secretary of the Council be had become convinced that Warrington was going to lag behind in labour matters unless they formed a centralized body. Efforts diffused over a number of bodies meant weakness and if they could getla central- ized body with a membership of all organized workers in the district they would forge ahead. The only politics for the worker and for his children were labour politics, and the proposal to federate was put forward with a view to their being able to build up a powerful labour force in Warrington, and if they could do that they would achieve such a success as would astonish, themselves. Nothing in Warrington could step bathe way of Norganixed labour. The minutes of the committee were approved. Messrs. Plumb, Plinston, Podmore, and Edge, and 'Councillor Stott were elected delegates to the proposed [conference.

£BO,OOO FOR OBSOLETE WARSIIIPS.

At Portsmouth on Tuesday some thirty obsolete war- ships were sold for just over £SO,OOO, or less than the cost of the smallest of them. The prices however, were considered good. Two well known training ships, the Formidable, t Bristol, and the Caledonia, at Queens- ferry, were also sold, and made £4,300 and £4,100 respectively.

WONDERFUL CURE Of SORE HANDS

After Most Awful Suffering Ever Ex- perienced Eight Doctors and Many Remedies Failed to Do a Cent's Worth of Good.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO CUTICURA _ IT IS ALL OVER NOW

`•I was troubled with sore liands, so sore that when I would put them in water the pap would nearly set me crazy, the skin would peel off and the flesh get hard and break, with blood flowing from at least fifty places on each hand. Words could never tell the suf- fering I endured for three years. I tried eight different doctors, but none did me any good, as my hands were as bad as when I began. I also tried many reme- dies, but none of them ever did me one cent's worth of good. I was discouraged and heart-sore. I would feel so bad mornings, to think I had to go to work and stand the pain for ten hours, I often felt like giving up my petition. I would have to wrap every finger up separately, so as to try and keep them soft, and then wear gloves over the rags to keep the grease from getting on my work. I had to wear gloves at night; in fact, all the time. But thanks to Cuticura, that is all over now. After doctoring for three years, and spending much money, a single box of Cuticura Ointment ended all my sufferings. It's been two years since I. used any, and I don't know what sore hands are now, and never lost a day's work while using Cuticura. Thomas A. Clancy, 310 North Montgomery St., Trenton, N. J."

THE EXAMINER.

JULY 14th 1806.

We wish somebody would THE NEW explain what possible good CONSCRIPTION the House of Lords can SCARE, hope to do by such a dis- cussion as that in which it indulged on Tuesday. Lord ROBERTS, who hese been Commander.in-Chief both in India and in England, told their lordships and the world at large not only that we are unprepared for war, but that with the exception of the South African case we have never been able to fight successfully on land unless we had the pc-operation of allies. Even South Africa, we ma remind Lord ROBERTS is not a wholly satisfactory exception to his rule, for despite the fact that he declared the war to be at an end when there was still a great deal of fighting to be done, it was eventually brought to a conclusion not by the exhaustion of the Boer forces, but by the promises made on behalf of this country at Veeregning. But what sort of patriotism is that which proclaims to the world, including, of course, all possible enemies, that we are not merely unable to defend our Country and our Empire, but that in the natural course of things we can never hope to be so? If we are really in such a condition as Lord ROBERTS declared to be the case, the proclamation of the fact from the housetops is nothing more nor less than an invitation to attack. The late Com- mander-in-Chief seems to be of opinion that we.. ought to maintain in times of peace such an army as would be required to carry on, at least in the earlier staged, any possible war. He underrates the value of our sea power in order to demonstrate the neceessity for a great and costly army. After indulging in much oratorical retro- spect of a somewhat questionable character, be trusted that he had been able to prove that in all the many wars we had waged, except the one with the Boers, our Treaties and subsidies had given us the aid we needed in support of sea power,and that without such aid sea power alone would have been an inadeqaate guarantee of our interests— indeed, that the fate of our sea pswer itself and our whole Empire would,ito say the least, have been problematical. The lessons of the Boer war, says Lord RoBERTS, seem to have been completely forgotten. No thought, he added, is given to the possibilities of the future, ansl the one prevailing idea seems to be to cut down our military expenditure without reference to our increased responsibilities and our largely-augmented revenue. The saying of such things as these would, in almost any case, be injudicious. Saying them at the time he did was unfair and untimely. We were waiting for the presentation of Mr. HALDANE'S scheme. It was expected to be a scheme paying regard not only to economy, but to increased effec- tiveness. Now that the sche.ne has been pre- sented, Lord ROBERTS will be able to sae with more accuracy whether any lessons hava been learned from the Boer war, and whether the cutting down of military expenditure is the sole thing that is considered in connection with the Army. In the meantime we may point out that Lord ROBERTS was himself sufficiently long at the Horse Guards to apply the lessons of the Boer war. Why, then, wore they not applied ? He was in a position to reorganise the Army on lines of thorough efficiency. Why, then, is he saying now, as he was saying a year since, that nothing has been done, that we are no more prepared for any large military operation than we were before the war in South Africa, and that, with a doubled expenditure on oer Army, we are virtually left naked to our enemies ? If this is the actual con- ' dition of things, Lord ROBERTS must not expect to escape responsibility. Of course we know what he is aiming at. He longs for conscription, in which longing he has the dangerous support of Lord Mume. It will be necessary to scare the country very badly indeed before any such devise as conscription is resorted to. But is it justifi- able ? Is it not almost treasonable, indeed, to try to scare the country in such a manner as is really 1 an invitation to our enemies ?

It was announced in the " Times " one dal that "The Chancellor of the Exchequor acknowledgeo the receipt of ;£l5 in Bank of England notes from D.F.' for additional income tax." No need to what D.F. stands for. Decided Fool, of course.

Members of the various ..EDERATION or Labour organisations in LABOUR. Warrington have just awakened to the soundness • elementary principle that " united we . divided we fall." The Trades Council and Labour Representation Committee are tedly on the decline in membership and , which fact jeopardises the future of the it party as a party in the town. This is to alored from the point of view of the party but perhaps the cause of Labour—and cause &ter than party—has not languished to the extent. -- In protecting child life the OTECTING, THE 'National Society for the CHILDREN. Prevention of Cruelty to Children is doing a noble , the full extent of which can hardly be :ed and the value of which cannot be over- iated. It is lamentable, hut nevertheless that the need for the society is as great as it was, and one fears to think what would ne of some children were it not for the good sof the workers of this great institution. At nnual meeting of the Warrington branch on aday the unpleasant fact had to be recorded increase in the number of cases dealt with y during the year. Inspector Cox has been exceedingly busy and a fitting tribute was to his excellent work. One gratifying feature tt financially the branch has been fairly well )rted, although the amount subscribed is not iy means commensurate with the usefulness a N.S.P.C.C. to the community. A.S to the ral and far-reaching operations of the Society ieed for additional inspectors is more con- ngly borne borne as time goes on. In the ested town districts there is such a constant m the intervention of the inspectors in cases treatment, that more remote quarters often )0 attention and it is imperative that the )ty's hands should be strengthened so that nate supervision over all areas can be exer- , • A suggestion made at Thursday's meeting, h is worthy of very serious consideration, is it special Government Department should be ed to watch over the interests of the children. hief functions would be to see to the rigorous rcement of all measures passed for the protec • of the little ones and to advocate:amendments e law to embrace classes of neglect which at mt are beyond the scope of the Society. questions as these ought to be kept prorni- ly before the public who, unfortunately, are to be apathetic when they are not brought direct touch with the evil conditions which uch a crying disgrace in too many homes.

-- One aspect of the result of TEA ON THE acceding to the demand flr TERRACE. women's suffrage has been overlooked. If the head-offices of a great ray company were invaded by summer umes and millinery, there would be more dents than are at present reported. muddle of the last Boer war will be as ling compared to the chaos when the mans arrive in 1910. --- Sir William Richmond has o°lTrsodpx.A.TioN. lately deplored the detri- mental effect which the TV and Scurry, especially in cities, has upon the sent age. Whereas a couple of generations ago grandfathers loved, when the day's work was to take a quiet, walk in the open air to :.esh the mind and get a little gentle exercise, modern young man scouts the idea of indulg- ina stroll along the beautiful Thames Embank i- nt n London. His opinion is that it "leads vhere." In this conjecture he is entirely mg. When, d.iring the day, the body has been escent, and the mind active, if the former, ing the evening, is given exercise while the ter takes a rest the walk acts as a compensator

many ills to which the flesh is heir. Action :cones exhausted and defective if it is not alter- Led with periods of contemplatioc. Such con- sting moods are neither contradictory nor !ompatible, but as mutually helpful to each ler as is the periodic note of rest in every mating string. Such recurring notes render ssible the production of beautiful harmonics, d the blending of harmonics engenders the rest musical harmony. Thus, a man who has d no rest at night becomes morose and a bad rker during the day. In tropical countries,when sun is high in the heavens, it is necessary to re a siesta. But in our modern Babylon the Cog man laughs at the idea of a siesta, and coo- lers that even a walk on the Embankment leads Where. Is that a fact ?

The Examiner, Saturday, July 14, 1906.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MOTOR ACCIDENT. During June the number of people summoned under the Motor-Car Act, 1903, was 357, of whom 324 were convicted-227 for exceeding the speed limit, 66 for driving recklessly, or in a manner dangerous to the public, and 31 for minor offences. There were 17 people killed and 37 injured, besides 24 other accidents resulting in damage to carriages, horses, or dogs. RUNCORN THEATRE BURNT DOWN. AN EXCITING SCENE. At half-past four on Nlonday afternoon the Theatre Royal, Runcorn, was discoveied to be on fire. At five o'clock it was a heap of burning rhins. Built principally of wood and half a century old, the building was quickly one mass of flames, and but for the promptitude with which four fire brigades were on the spot much other property would have been destroyed. It was surrounded on all sides by public offices, and dwellings, and the greatest excitement and confusion prevailed. Furniture was thrown into the street for removal. The offices of Mr. Samuel Taylor' ex-chairman of I Runcorn Council, caught fire, and the books were removed. The windows of the Derby Arms hotel were 1 broken by the heat. A possible explosion was averted by the heroism of a young man named Joseph Morris, who entered the burning theatre at great risk and removed two cylinders of oxygen. The whole of the scenery was destroyed, and Mr. Neville, of "The Silent Witness," has lost all his properties. The amount of damage is not yet estimated. Much sympathy is felt for Mr. William Caddick, who has been the popular and respected lessee of the theatre for over twenty years. The value of the Transporter Bridge across the Mersey , was demonstrated, as the Widnes steam fire engine , crossed and was the first on the scene.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The Cost of Vaccination. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there were present :—Mr. A. R. Norman (presiding), Admiral Jones-Parry, Messrs. G. Pilling, T. Wright, C. E. Linaker, jun., S. Woodward, C. Roylance, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, H. Eyes, J. Spruce, T. Guest, A. G. Guest, J. H. Davies, T. Baxter, P. Collins, J. Morris, J. Wood, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), Cook, De Prez' Pearson (relieving officers), and J. Cunnah (master). THE COST OF VACCINATION. Mr. Ernest Williams, of the Union Offices, Leigh, secretary to the committee of the Vaccination Confer- ence forwarded a copy of the proceedings of the com- mittee which showed that the president of the Local Government Board had declined to receive the com- mittee but added that the cost of vaccination was receiving his careful consideration. Mr. J. F. L. Brunner M.P., for Leigh. reported to the committee the result of his interview with the president of the Local !Government Board. The committee thereupon placed upon record their" satisfaction with the statement that the president of the Local Government Board is of ' opinion that the cost of public vaccination is excessive and is glad to learn that the subject is receiving his careful attention." They also resolved "That this committee representing 120 Boards of Guardians regard with regret the statement that the president of the Local Government Board, does not consider it necessary to receive a deputation, and hereby affirms its opinion that the president should receive such a deputation at an early date with a view to the question which the committee regard as important, and I urgent, being discussed with him.' Mr. Brunner under- took to again see the president and to urge that he I should receive the committee. Mr. Williams in a cover- ' ing letter said the committee were determined to impress upon the president the necessity of amending , the Local Government Board order of the 18th October , 1898 without further delay. They did not intend that the question should b• shelved and in the event of Mr. ! Burns failing within a reasonable time to submit a scheme by which Boards of Guardians were relieved to some extent of the cost of vaccination they proposed !to suggest another means whereby the object could be lobtained. The co:umittee appealed to the uncorn Board to make a grant towards the expenses of the con- ference. The Clerk stated that he doubted whether the auditor would allow the Board to contribute towards the expenses. It was decided to allow the letter to lie on the table.

APPOINTMENTS. Mr. Antwis was appointed a member of the Assess- ment Committee to fill the vacancy caused by the death of Mr. John Smith, Grappenhall. Miss Jessie Clare, Preston Brook, was appointed industrial trainer at the Workhouse. THE COST OF OUT-RELIEF. The Finance Committee reported the payment in out- relief of £7O to Mr. Cook, £2ii to Mr. de Prez and £2S to Mr. Pearson. DISABLED SEAMEN. The Parliamentary Committee recommended the adoption of the following resolution :—" That this Board circularise other Boards asking them to support the petitioning of the Government that the admiralty ' may take upon themselves the charge of disabled sailors and marines who have been disabled in 11. 1. service." Mr. Morris: I sea the Parliamentary Committee was resurrected this morning. The recommendation was approved.

* * * The Warrington Technical School may not only be a good training ground from a scholastic point of view, but it is also demonstrating that the pupils attached to the school are receiving excellent athletic training. Every fine summer evening and also every Wednesday and Saturday afternoon the pupils are to be seen disporting themselves at their ground near Padgate Station engaged either at cricket or lawn tennis. Even the girls are proving themselves adept at both games, for only this week, they defeated Penketh at cricket, and also Fairfield School at lawn tennis.

Police it I.L.P. •

SINGULAS PROCEEDINGS AT WIDNES.

Seddon on Downtrodden Englishmen. Tremendous feeling against the action of the police in prosecuting members of the local branch of the Independent Labour party for using Victoria-square for their meetings is rampant in the town of Widnes, and fully 1,500 congregated on Saturday evening to uphold their rights of free speech. Os Thursday last seventeen members were arraigned before Messrs. Piptington and Gossage, and fined for obstructing the highway. The proceedings were taken under Section 72 of the Towns Highway Act of 1835. The party contend that they create no obstruction, and that it is only upon the complaint of one individual that the proceedings were taken. At seven o'clock Mr. Standring (St. Helens) moved from the Labour rooms to the big lamp in the square. Addressing the huge crowd, he said they were there that night to maintain those liberties for which Englishmen had fought and suffered in the past. He thought that the days to use the iron hand of autocracy to oppress people ha,d long gone by, but it seemed they had not. They were a peace-loving people, who had no desire to quarrel, either with police or with others, or with those who pulled the strings behind the police. All that was wanted was to do that which was right. The party had come to stay in power in Widnes, and also to claim the right to hold their meetings in a square of th it I character. English laws, if put into practice, would do away with all the liberties they enjoyed. At this juncture Superintendent Strickland and his men, who had been standing on the fringe of the crowd, came forward and asked the leaders to disperse, but upon resisting their names were taken. Mr. Seddon, M.P., handed in his card, but intimated that he would not move. Several other names were taken, the crowd showing their dissatisfaption at this course by hooting. Content with the names taken, the police dispersed. Mr. Seddon now rose, and from a stool in loud tones denounced the action of the police. He said be was an ALP., and not a law breaker. They were there that night to defend the right which had often caused the shedding of innocent blood. They were guilty of a technical offence, but those who were responsible for the proceedings against them had souls as narrow as they could possibly be, and they might as well bounder the Dania of Russia as under the English liberty-loving flag. Referring to the reverend gentleman who first made the complaint against their meetings, and who was the cause of the quarrel, because they interfered with his religious services, he on being interviewed had said with childlike innocence that he was a Conservative and did not like their politics, ail had set the mighty arm of the law into operation to help him to carry out his petty spite. For every one of the party who went down for the honour of the cause 100 would spring up. The speaker would come every Saturday night and give his name willingly if they persisted in carrying out the struggle to the bitter end ; and he did not think there was one amongst them who would condescend to have their liberty while the right of free speech was taken from them. (Lond cheers and cries of" No.") Mr. Strandring, after making a collection, stated that they would hold their meeting on the prohibited spot on Sunday as usual.

Sunday's Proceedings. 1 MORE NAMES TAKEN.—A SPEAKER ARRESTED The news of what occurred the night before did not draw half the crowd that was anticipated on Sunday afternoon. Promptly at three o'clock,after the men's I Bible class had dispersed from St. Paul's Church, mem- bers of the Labour party moved to the big lamp in Victoria-square, when Mr. Dan Garghen took the chair. Addressing the crowd that kad assembled, more out of curiosity than anything else, he said he had volunteered to take the chair, and, if needs be, to pay the penalty for obstruction by going to prison. There were no goods and chattels to seize at his house, and he was fully prepared for anything before being robbed of free , speech. It was something cruel the way they had been treated. Parliament had the matter before them, and at no distant date the case would be threshed out on the floor of the House of Commons. (Hear, hear.) Mr. F. W. Lawler Manchester U.K.A., by rising to speak gave the signal to the police to commence their operations. Superintendent Strickland, Inspector Pound, and Sergeants Davidson and Blackball now thrust their way through the crowd and requested the speakers to move on. This they declined to do, and their names and addresses were taken. The officers' attention was afterwards turned to those in the crowd who would not leave, and names were taken wholesale. Satisfied with this, the police paraded the fringe of the crowd and listened to the views of the speaker. It was far from his desire, he said, to come into conflict with the police, but they must fight hard against any of their liberties being taken from them. In lthe evening, after eight o'clock, a tremendous crowd gathered, and was addressed by Mr. Despres, of Liverpool. Speaking at the lamp, he asked them to come close together, and keep within fifteen yards of the parapet, which was the recognised limit, and if they were molested by the police they were at liberty to punch them under the jaw. The police had done their level best to cause incitement of a breach of the peace. He exlaorted them to stand firmly together, and also asked them to come and be witnesses at his trial next week. He would pay no fine whatever but would suffer imprisonment. The speaker then, in no measured terms, gave his opinion of Superintendent Strickland's capacity as a police officer. Superintendent Strickland then came forward and asked for the speaker's name, at the same time saying he had no intention of arresting him. Mr. Despres said if there was any troubte it was clue to the police, and, he added to the superintendent, "Don't you attempt to remove this crowd; if you do you will have a hot job." It was the duty of the police to suppress crime, and not to incite the populace. Subseeuently the names of some of those present . were taken, and then Mr. Despres proceeded to take up a collection in aid of a defence fund. While this was , in progress ke was arrested, and the crowd were dis- persed by a posse of police. Mr. Despres was detained at the police office for about a quarter of an hour. ALLEGED THREATS TO A PRESSMAN. ' At the Widnes Police Court, Mr. S. H. R. Scales, a reporter of a local newspaper was granted a summons against a Widnes constable for using threatening language. Mr. Scales stated that he visited the police station on Sunday, after the labour disturbance, in the course of his ordinary duty, but was refused permission to enter, and be alleged that one of the constables said if he did not go away he would throw him into the gutter. He took this action as a protest. The Mayor said he was disposed to allow the matter Ito be threshed out, and granted the summons.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Mr. Joseph Mercer, of Rydal Mount, Dough Green, near Widnes, has several giant roses out now 18lin. in circumference, and nin. deep. * * Our congratulations to Mr. H. Ashton, the popular secretary of the Warrinc,-tm. P.C., on being elected vice-president of the Northern Rugby Union ! * * * Retribution has fallen upon Johannesburg t the Stock Exchange bids fair to close, and the gamblers now only count their losses.—R. L. Outhwaite, in the " Daily News."

* * We hear a rumour that Mr. John White, about to retire from the presidency of the Warrington Conservative Association and that he will be succeeded by Mr. W. Peter Rylands. * * The Trades Council sensibly proposes an amalgama- tion of all labour and trades union organisations in the town. We offer a title to the new body, if formed—i.e. the Warrington Labour Federation. * The following recently appeared in an American newspaper :—" Six men were seriously injured by the collapse of a bride on the Brednardine, Man., line of the C.P.R." A somewhat similar one occurred in an auditor's report when an item appeared :—" H. Baldwin, repairing bride, g,5." • * It will be remembered that at the last Council meet- ing a proposition to pay Messrs, Edelsten £2,000 on account for the purchase of the "pm mill " was rejected by a majority of the Council t,h,iugh the Mayor said they were threatened with a writ. We hear that the writ has arrived ! * 5 * Trades Councillor Corrin is not far wrong when he says if it were not for the local press there would be no Trades Council at all. The Council has been kindly treated by the local newspapers and the " Examiner" may fairly claim to be the pioneer in giving it the benefits of publicity. Mr. Gladstone admitted in the House of Commons on Monday that as one result of the tram ticket nuisance a child had been killed at Hammersmith, two other fatal accidents had occurred, and there had been seven accidents involving personal injury. The legality of these advertisements is now under consideration." * * * When the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday appointed a deputation to visit children's institutions, the chairman created some amusement by remarking: " Where shall we send them—to Poplar We hardly think, however, the establishments of this Union which has gained such notoriety of late will form the destination of the Warrington deputation. * * * A piece of paper bearing the following was found in the pocket of a man who died suddenly in a West End street : There is so much good in the worst of us, And so much bad in the best of us, That it behoves none of us To talk about the rest of us. • * * Warrington cyclists had a grand time at Beeston on Saturday and Mr. John Naylor must have been gratified to see how highly his generosity in inviting the wheelers to his beautiful residence is appreciated. It was fitting that the occasion should not be allowed to pass without feeling references to the late Mr. "Pa" Davies who was such a familiar figure at these outings which have now extended over fourteen years. * * The " friends of voluntary schools, Churchpeople, and others of the parish of Thelwall " have protested against the Education Bill. The protest was azongly backed by the village stationmaster, who elicited "laughter and applause " (vide press) by the wise dictum that "the sooner Mr. Birrell put the Educa- tion Bill into the waste-paper basket the better." Would it be " the better" for the Bill or the basket? * * * The remarkable performance of Fielder in the Gentlemen v. Players match on Monday aptly marked the hundredth anniversary of this fixture. For one bowler to take all ten of his opponents' wickets is an exceedingly rare performance in first-class cricket. It has not been done for about six years in England ; only four times in the whole history of Lord's, and on Monday for the first time in the Gentlemen v. Players match.

* * * A meeting of the General Purposes Committee was held on Wednesday to consider the question of pur- chasing some property in St. James-street, but not enough members to form a quorum turned up. Con- sequently nothing was done, and at the sale later on in the day the property in question was withdrawn. There, is good ground for supposing that the mem- bers of the Town Council have had more than enough of property purchasing just lately. * * . The teacher was trying to impart a lesson on the circulation of the blood. " Now boys," he said, "if I stood on my heal the blood, as you know, would run into it, and I should turn very red in the face." " Yes, sir," shouted all the class. " Well, then," continued the teacher, r` what I want you to tell me is this : How is it that while lam standing upright in the ordinary way the blood doesn't run into my feet ?" One small boy answered immediately, "Why, sir, because yer feet ain't empty !" And the teacher was very annoyed.

The recent conference for the cult Of the beautiful in industrial centres, held in Manchester, proved so suc- cessful that it is intended to hold another next year. The committee have not yet decided in which town the conference will meet. The .Hon. Secretary, the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson, of the Training College, Warrington, would welcome suggestions on this point. It is proposed to change the title of the conference to that of "The City Beautiful," which, it is thought, will embrace the work of all societies concerned in the betterment and beautifying of our towns. LAWN TENNIS, CROQUET, and other outdoor sports.—J. S. GARLAND, 51,Bridge-st.—Advt. Warrington Man's Strange Conduct. An extraordinary affair happened at Llangollen on Saturday. It appears that shortly after midnight a man, who gave his name as Bert Smith, of Moore, near Warrington, was taken to the police station. He had been found wandering about near the river at Trevor, and a river watcher took charge of him. When questioned, he could give no coherent account of himself, but muttered : Ido not know My mind is a blank since Tuesday !" He was seen by the , police surgeon, and then locked up for the night. He was to be taken before the Bench in the afternoon, and requested permission to wash himself. Alarmed by hearing gurling sounds, P.S. Wyse forced an entrance to the lavatory, the door of which had been secured, and found the man with his head in the water. When lifted out Smith was unconscious. Medical aid was summoned, but a considerable time elaspsed before he could be removed to the local hospital on a stretcher. Had the police been a minute..dater in forcing an entry the medical opinion is that the man would have succumbed. Smith was brought before the magistrates on Monday, and, after consultation with a doctor, and upon the understanding that he would be placed under medical supervision he was discharged. LAWN MowEits' Garden Tools, ac. Splendid selection.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-st.—Advt. Death of Mr. William Brookes. We record with regret the death which took place at his residence, 339, Manchester-road, on Sunday afternoon, of Mr. William Brookes who for many years had carried on the business of stationer, accountant and lithographer in Queen-street. The deceased gentleman, who was 74 years of age, suffered from bronchitis and heart troubles for some time. He was one of the managers of Hamilton-street schools and a regular worshipper at the Parish Church. He leaves a widow, and four sons and three daughters to mourn his logs —The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon, after a special service had been held at the Parish Church. The officiating clergmau was the Rev. Canon Willis, assisted by the Rev. T. A. J. Ridpath. The chief mourners were : Messrs. W. A. Brookes, Samuel H. Brookes, and J. A. Brookes (sons), the Misses Frances, Ella and Marian Brookes (daughters), Mrs. S. H. Brookes (daughter- in-law), Mr. John Brookes, Manchester, and Mr. Edwin Brookes, Bolton (brothers), Mrs. Cuppage (sister), Mr. Alec Cawley, of Tylciesley (brother-in-law), ~anad Mrs Cawley, Miss Edith Unsworth, Cadishead (n4ece), Mr. John Cawley, Glazebrook (brother-in-law), Mr. W. S.GARLANO,SI to 55, Bridge-st.—Advt Claim for Ground Rant. —Peculiar Case. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C. Robert Isher- wood, Lovely-lane, sued Thomas Hardman, general dealer, Longford-street, for the sum of Ll 9s. 10d., being half-year's ground rent for some land off Longford-street.—Mr. A. Browne, for Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co., stated that the plaintiff purchased some ground rents from the trustee in bankruptcy of Thomas Patrick McCue, one of which was the present amount claimed. The defendant had consulted Messrs. Kelly and Picton, ol St. Helens, with the result that they had brought in John Peacock, of Mersey-street,as a third party. It was quite true that Hardman assigned the lease of the ground to Peacock, and it could not be discovered what defence there was to the action, inas- much as the defendant was primarily liable in his original covenant to the original lessee.—lt was then proved by Mr. Isherwood that he purchased the ground rent, and that it was due.—Mr. Baines, from the office of Messrs. Greenall and Co., produced the assignment of the property.—Defendart explained to his Honour that he thought he had done altogether with the land and that be was not liable. —Mr. Peacock, the third party, said he had sold the land to Mr. Dolan, who had gone to Africa.—His Honour pointed out that Dolan was liable to Peacock, and the latter was liable to Hardman, who was liable to the plaintiff.— A verdict was given for the plaintiff for the amount claimed with costs.

IThe current issue of " Chatter" conntains an , interesting biographical sketch, with portrait, of Mr. i Frank Dunne, the well-known Warrington draughts I player. During the last 30 years Mr. Dunne has been , one of the best-known exponents of the game in Britain, and has met nearly all the champions. He was the founder of the Yorks Draughts Association, and one of the f-unclers of the' English Draughts Association. Mr. Dunne also organized the Warring- ton Draughts League, and was presented with the I " Chalmers " Cup when it came into the possession of the Warrington Club. •Fle was born in Yorks in ' 1853.

Local Art Student's Suscess. Among the five persons in the United Kingdom who. have this year succeeded in winning the diploma of' the Royal College of art, is Miss G-. Pickavance, of Warrington. The success entitles Miss Pickavance to add to add to her name the letters A.R.C.A. and to wear the cap and gown. Miss Pickavance was formerly a ctudent at the Warrington School of Art where she obtained her art class teachers's certificate and became a pupil teacher at the school a. position which she held until she entered the Royal College as a free student. In 1901 she won a scholar- ship to the college valued at £6O a year and tenable for a number of years. Liberal Picnic to Southport. To-morrow (Saturday) will witness the holding of a picnic to Southport which for size easily establishes a, local record. It has been arranged in connection with the Warrington Liberal Association, Woman's Liberal Association, Junior Liberal Association and Progres- sive Labour League. The first of the special trains which has hen chartered will leave the Central station at 12.10 and other trains will leave at intervals up to 2 o'clock. In the afternoon tea will be served 'in the Winter gardens after which a short meeting will be held. The return journey.will be commenced at 8.30 and trains will leave at intervals from that hour until 10.30.

The half-yearly report of the Manchester and Liver- pool District Banking Company, which will be pre- sented at a meeting of shareholders to be held at Man- chester on Wednesday next, discloses a very flourishing state of affairs. After allowing for all bad and doubt- ful debt; there is a gross profit on the half-year's working of £228,697 145.3 d. to which has to be added the sum of £37,095 Os. Bd. brought forward from the 30th December. Deducting working ettacenses and pro- vision for income tax there is left available for dividend £179,068 Os. 2d. The directors recommended the payment to the shareholders of 21s. per share which will absorb £131,250, and propose to deal with the balance by placing £lO,OOO to the reserve fund and carrying forward £37,818 Os. 2d. The reserve fund with the above addition will amount to £1,835.000. The subscribed capital of the company is £7,000,000 and the capital paid up £1,500,000. BABY CARRIAGES. Latest patterus.—.T. S. \ GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrmgton.—Advt. Congress of Chambers of Commerce. Councillor J. Fulton Smith, the president, and I Alderman Bennett,. the hon. secretary of the War- I rington Chamber of Commerce, were appointed to represent the Warrington Chamber at the sixth Con- gress of the Chambers of Commerce of the Empire, ' which was held on Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday last in the Grocers' Hall, London. Councillor J. Fulton Smith was unavoidably prevented from attend- ing. The Warrington Chamber had given notice of the following resolution : " That this Congress is strongly impressed with the desirability of appointing lan Imperial Council, representing all the main divisions of the Empire, with the object of promoting defatite mutual consultation as to matters of imperial interest and devising measures calculated to unite the Mother Country and the Colonies for purposes of commerce and defence." In order to save time, Alderman Bennett withdrew the resolution and seconded a resolution on the same subject which had been moved by Mr. Richard H. Alexander, on behalf of the Vancouver Board of Trade, and read as under : "That this Congress urges upon his Majesty's Govern- ment and upon the Governments of the Colonies the appointment of an Imperial Council to consider questions of Imperial interests, especially those tending to promote international trade." The resolu- tion was carried with only two or three dissentients. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—IINDER 'rats CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt.

Loyal. Garibaldi Lodge National 1.0. Odd- yellows. The half yearly meeting of the above was held on Saturday at the Blue Lion Inn, Market Place. A cordial welcome was extended to the Grand Master Councillor Wright. The Noble Grand, Bro. Perry, conducted the meeting. The balance sheet for the half year showed a heavy sick rate, having paid £Ol Os. 2d. for sickness alone, this amount being larger than those paid for any previous half year, yet the Lodge had a gain of over £4 the total worth being at June 30th over ;£1,012. An increase of two members was reported ; total members on Books 186. An appeal from the Warrington District Nurses was com- mented upon and it was decided to support them, but that the business be left with District. It was also reported that the district had accepted August 18th for visit to Manchester brethren. SHOCKING MOTOR FATALITY. -- Girl Cyclist Killed by a Warrington

Car.

The West Cheshire Coroner was on Thursday notified of a fatal motoring accident at Spurstow. A. young woman named Lucy Waring, servant at the farm of Mr. Alfred Bateman, of Chorley, was instantly killed at a point in Spurstow where the Nantwich to Peckforton road crosses that from Whitchurch to- Tarporley about nine o'clock on Wednesday night by collision with a motor car,

Deceased had been to Spurstow Post Office on her bicycleond on the return :journey she encountercd the car at the cross roads. She had only recently-learnt to ride, and became nervous, with the result that she ran right in front of the car. In the collision the. bicycle was dragged under the car and mangled. From inquiries we laarn that the car was numbellk E.D.100, and driven by the owner Mr. X. AV. Wardle 275, dintitsforcl-road, 'Latchford. An inquest will be held to-day (Friday).

ROYAL MANCHESTER COLLEGE OF MUSIC

Local Students.

At the annual examination concerts at the Man cheater College of Music given on Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday in the Concert Hall, Ducie-street, two Warrington students have taken prominent part. We were privileged to attend Wednesday evening's concert end listened to a programme of vocal and instrumental music at the instance of the college pupils which in point of interest as well as in the merits of its per- formance was en agreeable revelation of the thorough- ness with which the musical training is pursued. Mrs. Arthur Booth and Miss Nellie Anderton (pupils of Miss Fillunger)each contributed a song'on Wednesday evening. Referring to these "The Manchester Guardian's" well- known critic writing above the initials " E.N." says :- 11n almost every respect last night's examination concert was an improvement on that of Tuesday. Mrs. , Arthur Booth's singing of Mendelssohn's " Hoar ye, Israel "revealed a clear and resonant voice that has evidently more behind it than the student's nervousness allowed ly-r to show. ,S.he would be justified in having . more coundence in herself. Caldares "Come raggio ; di sol "demands a breadth and confidence of phrasing that Miss Nellie Anderton hardly gave it ; but her ! performance in other respects was admirable. Her voice has a good deal of depth and colour in it.

Kay (21), I P. Chalmers (14), H. Harding (12) and H. Beaman (10). I The final was productive of a good race and Iwas won by R. Chalmers (1), Kay (2) and Mellor (3). Rudd (12), H. Sisson (12) and C. Turner (27). The final was hotly contested and Sutton was only a foot in advance of Rudd. Sisson was third, being a few inches behind Rudd. In the first half of the polo match the holders of the championship (Cheshire) defended the shallow end, and early on Wilcigoose was ordered out of the bath for standing. The ball next went to Swift who scored with \ a shot which Wilkinson only succeeded in touching with the tips of his fingers. The return of Wildgoose did not improve the Cheshire position and Swift was only robbed as he was about to shi.,,t. Swift was mit to be denied and later scored in the corner of the net. Half- time : Lancashire, two goals ' • Cheshire, nil. Re-starting, Cheshire asserted themselves, and Smith was early tested, Wilkinson in particular sending in some fine shots. Lancashire were being overplayed, but Cheshire could not find Smith napping, and tide was called with the score unaltered. 1 Lancashire are to he congratulated on a fine victory, I but in a sense Cheshire were robbed, as on two occa- sions the whistle was blown for a foul aginst 'them only a fraction of a second before goals were scored,

FATTTER. O TiiE "SPREE "—CITELDREN NEGLECTED. The maximum sentence of three months' hard labour was imposed on Peter Hares, Plumpton-street, at the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, for running away from the borough on the 15th May and leaving his three children chargeable to the Union.—Mr. B. Shearer who prosecuted said prisoner went on the "spree" and left the town on the 18th May and did not return until that week. On Monday he drew pension money to the amount of £2 ss. Bd. and had spent it all. He had lost his work through his own neglect.—Superintendent Monaghan said that was prisoner's eighth appearance.— Dr. Fox, who was on the Bench, said prisoner was dis charged from the White Lead Works through drunken- ness and was a very bad character. His house was in a disgraceful condition. -

- EMBEZZLEMENT BY Mri.xmrs. At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. J. W Hayward (in the chair) and other magistrates, Albert Baker. Priestley-street,•was charged with embez- zling sums of 41. 3d. and lid. received by him on account of his employers, Messrs. Whitlow and Darby- shire, Farmers' Stores Dairy.—Mr. Browne prosecuted and explained that Baker was engaged as a milk sales- man, his duties being to takeout milk.receive payments and debit customers who had milk with the cost by entering it in a book. Owing to some irregularity he was discharged but through the intervention of the Rev Father Swarbreck he was re-engaged. The previous Sunday he took out milk and disappeared without handing in the cash or his book. The result was that the prosecutors did not know how the accounts stood until prisoner was arrested. It was conjectured that the total amount involved was from 'Ds. to 36s.—Evidence as to payments which prisoner received for milk and did not hand in, was given by Mrs. Garston, 11, Dixon. street. Mrs. Welsby, 3), White-street, and Mrs. Board- Man,Thynne-street.—Prisoner denied any intention of dafrauding.—James Nolan, Earle-street. was then charged with embezzling ss. 61—Similar evidence having been given, the magistrates fined Baker 20s. and costs, or a month's imprisonment, and Nolan 10s. and costs or 14 days.
