SAOTLINTINS AT WARRINGTON. At the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday, Florence Wilkinson, a housekeeper, residiaz in Winwick-road, was charged with stealing from the shop of Mr. Walter Sutton, Market-street, on Monday afternoon, 12 yards of oil baize, of the value of 103., and also 'with stealing from the shop of Miss Charlotte Woods, Rylands- street, the same afternoon, two lace collarettes, of the valne of 45 sd. —Superintendent Monaghan stated that the prisoner was seen loitering outside the shop of Mr. Sutton at about six o'clock on Monday afternoon by a man named Cummins. He became suspicious, and watched her take the oil baize (produced) and go down Market-street. Cummins went inside the shop and told an assistant named John Burke, who followed her and caught her in Bewssyy-street. With regard to the second charge, prisoner entered the shop of Miss Woods Rylands-street, about four o'clock the same afternoon, and asked to be shown some collarettes. Miss Hough placed about twenty on the counter, but prisoner did not purchase any and left the shop. When she was being brought back to Mr. Sutton's shop, she deopped one of the col larettes, and Sergeant Evans found the other one in her possession. When she VMS charged she replied "I don't remember taking them." Superintendent Monaghan said the prisoner had bean before the Court on many occasions and had been fined twine this year for di nnkenness. The Bench sentenced prisoner to one month's imprisonment. EMBITI.ZI.EMENT AT STOCKTON TIFATIT. At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Samuel Kay Spencer was charged with embezzling the sums of 6s. 9d.. 4s. 4d. and 3s. the moneys of his employers Messrs. R. White ' and Sons, Ltd, mineral water manu- facturers, Stockton Heath.—Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the firm, and said the prisoner's duty was to take out mineral ‘vaters and return each evening the -moneys which had been received from customers. On Saturday, June Pith, he returned from his journey and banded to Zr. Parker, the foreman, a list showing he had given credit for ..c."l 19s. 101. It was thought to be all right, but as he did not turn up on the following Monday or Tuesday morning, and as he had gone away without' notice, the firm asked for a warrant, an' it was granted. Incpurles were made and it was found that prisoner had been round on the Monday morning end had drawn the sums of money. This was not the first ease of the kind which had arisen in connection with other men, and Messrs. White were, therefore, bound to press for a conviction. The prisoner's wages for the past seven weeks had averaged .£1 Ms. 11d.—Evidence was given by the customers as to the payments to the prisoner.—Defendant, in the course of a long state- ment, said that money was (Ming to him from the firm which would more than counterbalance the three sums which had been named; whether the customers paid him or not he had to pay the firm, and twice previously he had not not got his money in on a Saturday night, and once he lost two days wanes in consequence. There was a " lot of credit out." He had no intention to defrakid whatever, and he stopped away from tho works because he had been " gacked."—The Chairman said there was a slackness about the affairs of the firm.—Mr. .Browne : The same rules are in force all over the kingdom. The firm have several places elsewhere, bat Warrington is the only place where there have been so many embezzlements,—Prisoner was committed to gaol fur 14 days with hard labour.

WALKER'S TRIP.

Delightful Day at Llandudno.

It is generally admitted that the annual trip in con- nection with Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Limited. !takes first place amongst local works outings. For , many years past one day out of every 365 has been , set apart by the firm on which they entertain their employees and right royally lo they discharge the functions of hosts. Nothing as left to the men except ;to enjoy tenaselves—and that is a matter of little difficulty. Even the poorest of them has no occasion to cast anxious glances at the family exchequer I wonder how the cost of the day is to met. Everything is free. Nayonore, the men are given pocket money lin addition to the tickets and to him who is careful the trip need not cost a penny. Naturally the event i' is much looked forward to and no sooner s one trip to be written on the annals of what is past than the participants begin joyfully to anticipate the next. In years gone by most of the seaside resorts in the north have been visited from tithe to time and pleasurable memories are associated with each visit, but probably Saturday's trip will live longest in the minds of those who joined in it, Whoever were responsible for the selection of Llandudno as the place at which to spend the day they are to be heartily congratulated, From early in the morning until late at night there was one continuous round of pleasure—pleasure under skies of the deepest blue and under the benign influence of a glorious sun, the warmth of whose rays was tempered by cool sea breezes. To tell the story of the day in the proper sequence of events, Warrington was left shortly before seven o'clock. The early riser in the country sings joyously of the beauty of the sunrise over the hills and of the glories of the morning air, and his more slothful brother of the city, awakened by his song, mumbles a grumbling agreement and turns over to resume his broken slumbers, preferring to lie abed until the last minute and then after a hasty breakfast to rush off to another day's toil. True,when occasion demands, he can, in search of pleasure, be up with the lark or even before that herald of the rising sun, bht this tax was not levied upon him on Saturday and to his secret satisfaction he had not to be about until the streets were aired. The first of the trippers arrived at the Central Station shortly after six o'clock and whiled away the tedium of waitingby singing and joking. By 6.45 a.m. 1,100 men, women and children had appeared on the scene and prompt to time these left the station in three special trains which had been, chartered. The run to Liverpool was quickly over and the trippers crossed to the landing stage where the St. Tudno and St. Elvies were in waiting. The two were moored side by side and were quickly boarded. The sight of the water raised the apprehension of sufferers from sea sickness and one was heard to advise another not to travel on the St. Elvies because she rolled if it was a bit rough. The admonition was wasted, as the surface of sea as nearly approached that of the proverbial mill pond as it possibly could. The St. Elvies moved off first And was given five minutes start the St. Tudno following in her wake, each with a load of merry-makers bent upon extracting the maximum of fan oat of everying. The sail to Llan- dudno was delightful, and was accomplished in good time, the St. Tudno arriving within a few minutes of her companion ship shortly after ten o'clock, and Llandudno,—peaceful and aristocratic Llandudno was invaded by a typical Lancashire crowd and perhaps not a little shocked by their boisterous levity. They swooped down upon the town with a cheer and a laugh, a good-humoured rollicking crowd who carried everything before them. What mattered it to them that a staid old dame should regard with a forbidding countenance the revels of the disturbers of her morning nap, or that an irate old gentleman should •be a little hustled through lack of room as they marched along the pier. They were out to enjoy themselves and meant to do so, and who knows but that the old dame and the irate gentleman secretly yearned for the days when they could enjoy a day trip. What to do next? was the question which immediately arose, but as though drawn by a magnet the visitors disappeared in the direction of the Marine Drive to re-appear in parties of threes and fours on the sides of the Great Orme. The nigger minstrels in the" Happy Valley" reaped a rich harvest, and perhaps never have their quips and jokes been welcomed with a greater gusto. Proprietors of pleasure vehicles had their hands full in catering sufficiently for the trippers, and boat proprietors and cafe managers improved the shining hour and drew into their pockets a fair share of the money which was taken from Warrington. At noon the "St. Tudno" was in waiting to convey those who desired to con- tinue the sail to Menai 'Bridge. It would be difficult to imagine a more attractive journey than this Throughout the shore is within a stone throw, andl some of Wales' most beautiful scenery is brought in.o view. As the well wooded straits,, with the sombre hued mountains in the background, appeared in sight, the prettiest spot of the journey was brought within the ken of the visitors and loudly praised. Several hours we' allowed on shore at Menai and were spent in visiting places of intetest in the neighbourhood. Generally speaking it must, however, be admitted that Ikfenai did not impress the majority of the trippers. True the glorious scenery was admired but ip the words of one of Walkersmen there was" nowt " else to do. Everywhere was quiet —ideal for a seeker after a rest cure but too quiet for the energetic Warringtonians, and when the time for departure arrived all were ready for the journey. Menai was left at 6.39 and the sail to Liverpool was equally as enjoyable as the outward passage. Nothing delights a sea voyager more than to have music on the deck and while the absence of the band (which sailed back on the ether boat) was felt it did not prove fatal and a glee Tarty was rapidly organized. Catchy end popular airs were quickly run through to the accom- panyment of laughter and cheers, but as the night drew on and the moon rose in all her beauty. over the Welsh hills the trippers were ,hushed and the airs became more solemn until, as New, Brighton was passed, the strains of Cardinal Sewman's hynam' "Lead kindly light," sung by all who were on deck swelled out over the waters. - -

At Warrington a large crowd awaited to welcome back the holiday mokers and cheers having been given for the firm they gradually dispersed. At the close of the luncheoli- on board to several friends of the firm, Mr. .Monro, who presided called upon Alderman Francomb, who proposed the health of the firm in a short but appropriate speech. Messrs. Peter Walker and Son were, he said, kind to their employes, and where they had good men and good masters a firm was bound to succeed. It was a pleasure to find that the weather had been so favour- able. The Chairman called upon Councillor Fulton Smith to reply and he did so. Ihe healths of the Chairman, Councillor Fulton Smith and Captain Potter were also drank with enthusiasm, each gentle- man making a suitable reply. The Brewery Band accompanied•the excursios and played admirable selections of music.

WIRRAL AND BIRKENHEAD SHOW.

The Wirral and Birkenhead Show, to be held on the 18th and 19th of July, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas Lonsdale,as announced in our columns,promises to be one of the best in the history of this old Society, and, given fine weather, the Council deserve a bumping gate, The whole of the shedding for implements, motcir cars, waggons, etc., have been taken for these and other exhibits. Many new implements and itnproveraents are being exhibited for the Society's medals. The pig and sheep entries are far in advance of previous years. The cattle entries are a particularly fine lot, including the champion also the reserve for the champion prize at the Royal Agricultural Society of England's show so recently held at Derby. Classes for Kerry cattle are included this year, and these very interest- ing and useful milk-producing little beasts should certainly be an attraction to visitors. The horse section, which at this Society's Sh 0 t is looked upon by breeders and the leading exhibitors throughout , the kingdom as one of the best in the country, has an ' extremely good entry, especially in the hackney and harness classes, and includes many noted champion prize-winners who will most for the first time. The winner of the champion prize at the great international show hold last week at the Hague, also several other prize-winners at this renowned Continental Horse Show are entered for competition. The dairy produce section is. as usual, well rezt-esented. The dogs , are not only more numerous than for many years, buti a large number of the most noted prize- , winners n the country are being exhibited, collies, I bull-does, fox and Irish terriers, St. Bernard?, Great Danes, Pomeranians, and other toy dogs being parti- cularly strong. Poultry and pigeons are not extremely numerous. but the majority of the leading exhibitors in both Scltland and England have entered birds for , competition, and the total entry in this section numbers neatly 2,000 exhibits. Classes for rabbits and cavies , have been included for the first time, and have obtained la splendid entry, which will be of additional interest to the younger visitors to the show.

The Examiner, SP!-,urday, July 14, 1906.

CYCLISTS AT BEESTON.

Glorious Weather and a Successful Outing.

•

Many delightful haunts have been visited by the members of the Warrington Cycling Club and happy hours have been spent amid rural surroundings in various parts of Cheshire and Lancashire, but it may safely be said that no outing organized by these enthusiastic wheelers has been more highly successful than the visit to Beeston Towers last Saturday. The esteemed President (Mr. John Naylor) once more invited the club to his picturesque retreat and dis- pensed that princely hospitality which it has been the privilege of the cyclists to enjoy on so many occasions. Mr. Naylor is himself a veteran cyclist and nothing affords him greater pleasure than to encourage the healthful pastime. Saturday was an ideal day for the fixture—the genial conditions which have borne known as " Naylor's weather " prevailing. There was brilliant sunshine and, during most of the after- noon, a clear blue sky, while a light breeze tempered the heat. Once or twice the clouds gathered threateningly, but they: soon blew over, and there was not the slightest rain. The Beeston outing has latterly been called "Ladies' Day" and a cordial invitation is given to members of the fair sex to attend. Ori Saturday the response was particularly good and, all told, the party numbered close on a hundred. A fair sprinkling travelled by rail via Chester but the majority went by road and their's was certainly the better choice. The country side wore its most charming garb and the run through the far-famed Delamere Forest, with its ever-changing beauties, gave the cyclists the opportunity of seeing Cheshire at its best. Beeston Towers is pleasantly situated on a hill and command a fine view. Hard by is the grim old castle of Beeston, and in the background the well-wooded Peckforton range of hills with Lord Tollemache's splendid mansion form a lovely picture. The visitors arrived about five o'clock and Mr. and Mrs, Nayloy were in waiting to receive them. After their twenty- one miles ride the wheelers were somewhat dust- stained but a wash and brush up made all spick and span. Then the Nests assembled on the well- appointed bowling green where an excellent tea was served, Afterwards they were at liberty 'to wander through the grounds and house and the carved wood- work, which is such a striking feature at Beeston Towers, was much admired. As an admirable wind- up to the visit an a/ fresco concert was held. The club is fortunate in having such a variety of excellent talent at its command and the programme was a most interesting one. Sons were given with taste and expression by Miss Fairhurst,Mr. F. Fleldhouse Mr. W. A. Clay, and Mr. B. Poole and were highly appreciated One exceptionally popular item was a fic ng by Mr. Fieldhouse—with a swinging chorus—in which original verses with special reference to the outing were introduced. Quintettes were effectly rendered by Messrs. C. Hoult,F.Fieldhouse,W.A. Clay,B. Poole and S. Hassall. A humourons recitation which created a great deal of mirth was given by Mr. E. A. Sanders. The accompanists were Miss Mullarcl and Mr. T- P. Thrown.

Opportunity was taken to accord a hearty vote of thanks to the host and hostess. This was proposed in fitting terms by Mr. G. Bowman (captain of the club) , who referred to the fact that that was tie fourteenth visit of the club to Beeson and expressed the grati- tude of the company to Mr. and Mrs. Naylor for their kind hospitality, He also made a touching reference to the late• Mr. Pa" Davies who was always such a cheery figure at these outings and said his death was a very great loss to the club. Mr. E. Pickavance (secretary) seconded and the motion was carried with loud cheers and the singing of " For he's a jolly good fellow." Mr. Naylor re§ponded, and said that if the visit to Beestou was a red letter day in the annals of the War-. rington Cycling Club it was also a red letter day at Beeston Towers, where they were only too pleased to welcome the cyclists. He briefly alluded to the late Mr. Davies who, he said, was a true cyclist and a true gentleman in every sense of the word. The concert over, the cyclists turned their faces homewards, and after another glorious ride through the forest Warrington was safely reached about 10.30. Those who travelled by train arrived home a little earlier, and all were agreed that the " Ladies' Day" of 1906 had been unexcelled in the history of the club.

MOTORISTS FINED.

Excessive Speed on Cheshire Roads.

Two motorists were on Tuesday fined at the Dares- bury Petty Sessions for driving their cars at an exces- sive speed. In the first case Robert Craig, Walton Lea, chauffeur to Sir Gilbert Greenall, was charged with driving a car in Walton-road, Stockton Heath, on May 31st at a dangerous speed.—Mr. Browne appeared for the defendant and pleaded not guilty. Walter Timperley, York-street, Latchford, Ernest Henderson, and William Hooper spoke to seeing the car pass along Walton-road. The speed was dangerous and was about 20 miles an hour.

James Whitley, grocer, and a member of the Run- corn Rural District Council, said he was in his house at 9 p.m. and he could see through the window what took place. He heard a noise and he naturally looked through the window, from which he could see a distance of 130 yards. The car travelled at a most terrific speed, and it went over a length of 100 yards in six or seven seconds, and he worked that out at the 100 yards distance at nearly 30 miles an hour. Ttere were 20 people on the roadway. They could not stand on the footpath because at that particular point it was only 4ft. wide. He considered the speed extraordinary for any motor car, especially to come round a continual curve. He felt convinced that, sooner or later, unless the excessive speed of motor cars was curtailed, there would be a very serious accident at the place in question. He could scarcely see the people for the tremendous dust which was raised in the road. Witness could not take the number because of the great speed of the car. In 150 yards there were about seven open- ings on to the highway, which made excessive driving more daugerous.—By Mr. Browne : He was only prejudiced against excessive speed. He. had a hatred of motor cars on account of the excessive speed and dust.

Sergeant Kennelley said he heard some one shout "look out "and he saw a motor car go by at a terrible speed. There were three cyclists in the road and one was riding immediately in front of the other two, and they had to get out of the way. Witness rushed into the road and got the number of the car. There were 20 or 3.0 people about, mostly children and young people. The number of the car was " L.C. 6752." Mr. Browne said the witness appeared to have assumed that the car was a racing one, but it was only a chassis, which had been brought from Derby to be tried. The defendant was driving to Walton Lea and was taking the car to Mr. Mnskett at Walton to have something done to some part of the wheels' and it was then going to betaken back to Birmingham to have the body of the car put on. The defendant and his friends were in no hurry, and were going through Stockton Heath at the same rate they had been going week after week for the last eight months. The witnesses had been misled by the appearance of the ear. He suggested that Mr. Whitley was biased, and that there was not a shadow of a doubt that he disliked all motor cars because they went too fast by his place, and created a nuisance. He contended that there had been an excess of zeal on the part of Mr. Whitley and the other witnesses. Although the speed might have been excessive there was no danger. The defendant said he had been drivihg motor cars for eight years, and during that time had covered qnite 100,000 miles. Witness was in no particular hurry to go home. He aid not see any one try to stop him. He was not going faster than he ordinarily did on the same road. It would be impossible to go round the corner in question at 30 miles an hour. He was only going at ROYAL PdANCIIESTER COLLEGE OF MUSIC. a* ordinary speed-15 or 20 miles on a straight piece.% In answer to the Chairman, the defendant said it was 60. horse-power machine. • Seth George, a youth in the employment of Sir , Gilbert Greenall, said the defendant was not going at .an excessive speed or at a speed that was dangerous., He did not see any one get out ed the way. i At the annual examination concerts at the Man- Leonard Clare, a youth, said there was no danger to any children. THE FREE CHURCHES.

BEY. F. B. MEYER'S VISITS.

The Rev. F. B. Meyer, 8.A., will, at the close of his year of office as President of the Baptist Union, devote himself to the work of the Free Church Councils throughout the country. He will give a Sunday and the four following days in each week. All applications for his services should be addressed to the Rev. Thomas Law, at the Memorial Hall, London, E.C. A plan of visitation will be made up as soon as possible, the earliest date for which Mr. Meyer can be booked Laing: September. 1907.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONGO ATROCITIES.

The Committee of the National Council has been carefully considering the subject of the Congo, and efforts will be made to co-operate with the Congo Reform Society in bringing the needs of that great country before the people of this land. It is probable that a der station will be arranged at an early, date to Sir Edward Grey.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOCIAL PROBLEM.

The Social Questions Committee of the National Free Church Council has in prepara- tion a volume which, if it is not epoch-making, will at least be of great interest and service. The volume, which has for its title "The Social Mission of the Church,", consists of contribu- tions by representative Free Church leaders, and deals with the attitude of the Church in regard to Social Questions. The editing of the volume has been undertaken by the Rev. C. Ensor Walters, whose long experience in Social Work peculiarly fits him for such a position. The volume, which will be published at a popular price, will be ready early in the autumn.

THE BRITISH CHAUTAUQUA.

The British Chautauqua is to be held at Aberystwyth as usual this year, and among the list of engagements we notice that Mrs. Benham, Secretary of the Girls' Guild, is to give an address 'on " Some Notable Cranks." OPEN AIR SERVICE REQUISITES.

The Publication Department of the National Council, Memorial Hall, now issues at very cheap prices Hymn Sheets (each containing 12 or 13 selected hymns), the Mission Hymnal, a booklet on Open Air Service' Organisation by Rev. F. B. Meyer, one on the necessary Methods and Training by Rev. C. Ensor Walters; book- lets for New Converts by Dr. Campbell Morgan, F. B. Meyer, Gipsy Smith, and J. Tolefree Parr. Pledge forms and worker's cards are also sup- plied at very cheap rates. The manager will gladly send specimens with detailed price list on receipt of a postcard.

HARROGATE GONVENTION.

• For two years running the' National Council has been holding meetings at well-known health resorts in the height of the summer season. These meetings have been eminently successful each year, and it is only natural that they should come to be regarded as a regular feature of Free Church Council work. tis felt in many quarters that the Free Church Council ought to hold an annual series of meetings of the nature of the Keswick Conventions. It has accordingly been arranged to hold a Convention at Harrogate somewhat on these lines. The date fixed is between Sundays, August 12 and 19, when the Harrogate season will be in full swing. Already Err. R. F. Horton, Rev. H. Elvet Lewis, Rev. F. B. Meyer, ti.A., and Gipsy Smith have arranged to be present and take part. The programme is not yet complete, but will include devotional sessions, conferences, sermons, and addresses definitely aiming at the deepening of spiritual life.

FREE CHURCH CAMPS FOR SCHOOLBOY& 1, This year four Schoolboys' Camps will be held : one each at Matlock and Bridport, and two at Middleton. Camp is 10 days of open air life free from all the trammels of conventionality in dress and other things. Four or five boys sleep in each bell tent, under the charge of an officer who is generally an Oxford or Cambridge man. During the day boys and officers are continually in each other's company. There are no Camp servants (save an Army cook); each tent takes its turn at orderly duty and learns the joys of washing up, and in the open air with Camp " crockery " the joys are great. There are no rules save one or two elementary ones necessary to the very existence of Camp, such as any boy would keep without knowledge of theme as indeed most do. The age limit is 14 (or 13 in the case of brothers) to 19, and any boy between these ages, even if he has left school, ranks as a "schoolboy." The bathing is always carefully looked after, and each camp boasts a doctor— to heal sunburns, and the few minor ills that flesh in Camp is heir to. Besides the games, Camp generally holds a Sports day, and at least one picnic. Boys are urged to bring songs or instruments for the "Sing-Song." an impromptu entertainment, abounding in mirth if not Always in artistic genius. All inquiries should be addressed to H. C. Few, BeiTycroft, Cambridge.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

Two .new leaflets have been issued by the National Free Church Connell, which ought to prove useful in counteractit'no• some of the curiously mistaken ideas about the present Education Bill. One is entitled "The Truth About the Education Bill." It deals with such cries as "Confiscation,' "Banishing the Bible," "Establishing a New Religion," "Teaching Nonconformity," &c., &c. The other, called " Parental Rights in Religious Education;' specially deals from a Nonconformist point of view with the clerical statement that the parent has an "inalienable right" to look to the State for dogmatic and denominatal teaching for his children. Incidently, it shows from a Parliamentary Return that under the old School Board system, so often referred to as 1` godless," there were only nine small places in England where no religious instruction was given. It also states that there are under the Act of 1902 no less than 5804 districts where the only existing school is the nroperty of the Anglican Church.

HOT WEATHER HEALTH HINTS.

Folks are really too careless in summer, and play pranks with their health which would horrify them in any other season of the year. Summer has its own special health-perils, all of which are aggravated by summer-recklessness. Heat plays havoc even with .the healthy. The long hours of sunshine, especially to the strenuous toiler, bring in their train nervous exhaustion sleeplessness, headaches, neuralgia, languor, and "that tired feeling" which fortells breakdown. The sudden changes of temperature so common in this climate often cause obstinate summer colds, catarrh, and influenza.

But if people would keep their vital functions in order by taking a Bile Bean at noori and a Bile Bean at night, they would escape all such evils.

To dyspeptice, bilious subjects, and people troubled with chronic ailments, summer has its own special terrors. Heat affects the liver and causes bilious head- aches, sickness, dizziness, and nausea. Heat often affects food, sometimes so slightly souring meat, milk, and other comestibles as to be scarcely perceptible by taste, but quite sufficiently to produce fermentation in the stomach, heartburn, wind-distension, colic, diarrheas, and other digestive troubles. But Bile Beaus will set stomach and liver right, and keep them healthy and vigorous. There's very Tittle sense in "going away for a change" when your stomach, liver, bowels, and kidneys are in open rebellion; for the change' in such a case, is just as likely to be a change for the worse as the better. The change you need is a constitutional change. such as is wrought by Bile Beans the great tonic alterative. An alterative—according to Ale dictionary—is" a medioine which works a constitutional change, restoring healthy functions." Bile Beans are a safe, non-mercurial, and purely vegetable alterative, possessing potent tonio properties, which act so gently and genially that they cause no discomfort, and so naturally that they cause no evil after-effects.

FOOTBALL.

Northern Rugby Union.

MEETING OF THE GENERAL COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the General Committee of the Northern Rugby Union was held in Manchester on Tuesday even- ing, Mr. J. H. Smith occupying the chair. The draw for the first round of the Northern Union Cup Qnalify- ing Competition was made, the local clubs being drawn together as follows : District 2.—Widnes Rangers v. Runcorn Highfield. Walker's Brewrey v. Widnes Rovers. Leigh Shamrocks v. Leigh Rangers. Wigan Highfield v. Pemberton. District 3.—Lancashire Dynamo v. Cadishead. Eger- ton v. Salford St. Bartholomew. The ties to be played on November 24. _

_ The following clubs were exempted from both qualify- ing and intermediate rounds : Leigh, Hunslet, Leeds, Oldham, Bradford, Neighley, Pilger,ll Kingston Rovers, Broughton Bangers, Halifax, Runcorn, Huddersfield, Swinton, St. Helens, Warrington, Wakefield Trinity, Hull, and Salford. The officials of the Northern Union for the ensuing year were elected as follows :—President, Mr. J. B. booke, Wakefield; vice-president, Mr. H. Ashton, Warrington ; hon. treasurer. Mr. J. Nichol], Halifax ; hon. secretary, Mr. Joseph Platt, Oldham. The hon. secretary reported the result of the voting in respect to making it compulsory that teams shall play six backs and seven forwards. The result was averse to the pro- posal, and the clubs will consequently constitute their teams as they choose. LANCASHIRE COUNTY UNION. The annual meeting of the above body was held in Manchester, on Tuesday evening, Mr. J. H. Houghton presiding. In submitting the annual report and balance sheet the Hon. Secretary, Mr. Joseph Platt, said that the statement of accounts for the past season showed a balance in hand of 04S.

For the first time since its inception the county championship was in abeyance. Lancashire met Cumberland twice for the purpose of deciding last season's county championship, but in each case a draw was registered,and honours therefore were divided. The report and balance sheet were adopted unanimously. It was stated that a county match had been arranged with Yorkshire on the same date as last year. The usual match with Cumberland was still in abeyance.

HARD TIMES FOR SKINS.

Heat Discomforts and Dangers— Zam-Buk a Cooling, Health-Giving

Balm.

The tale of Summer's heat and glare will be told in all sorts of skin sicknesses, from simple sun spots and peeling noses to more serious disorders, arising from excessive persiration or dryness. Bands, faces, and necks are, of course, the parts liable to suffer from disfiguring sunburns or smarting patches. In the armpits and other joints serious sores ma l arise from chafing and excessive perspiration, and on the chest and back heat rashes or eruptions are very pre- valent. Light clnthing which permits the skin to " breathe " freely frequent ablutions, and regular anointings with Zam-Buk, the balm that just suits the season and the skin, are absolutely essential to skin-health just now. Zam-Buk is delightfully cooling and soothing. The pure skin medicines contained in its herbal essences are so refined, and of such scientifically-accurate con- sistency, that they soak through the tissues, and restore the softness, clearness, and pleasant "feel " of health to the most tioublesome skip

Zam*Buk is both a protective and a corrective balm, and obstinate rashes or sores yield to its healing infln- ems() just rs surely, if not as quickly, as do the minor trials of the season. Miss Mary Baker, of 27, Elammarton-road, Northfleet, Kent, writes :—" Last summer my brother got very badly sunburnt, and bis face broke out in nasty sores. We tried all sorts of ointments, and he also attended the hospital, but the sores spread. At last we tried Zam-Buk and it completely cured him. Miss Walters, of 5, Meads Street, Eastbou'rne, writes : —" An irritating rash broke out on my back and chest. bathed the parts in warm water, and afterwards dressed them with Zara-Buk. I got speedy relief, and after continuing the applications for a few days all the spots quite disappeared. My skin is, now perfectly clear.' Keep a box in your bag for the holidays, and on a handy shelf at home.

WAS HE A LUNATIC? SENSATIONAL ESCAPE FROM RAINHILL.

About a week ago one of the inmates of Rainhill Asylum escaped, and from the extraordinary efforts that were made to trace his whereabouts people have been led to believe that the circumstances of the escape were somewhat unusual. It is now stated that the alleged lunatic, who was confined in the danger quarter on account of his previous histery,escaped by removing his cell window completely out of its place, and that he left the institution without a stitch of clothing, not even a shirt. The story is that the lunatic was an expert burglar who had been pronounced insane by the medical man in charge of one of his Majesty's prisons. The burglar was undergoing a sentence of a con- siderable period, and it is believed that his madness was simply cleverly feigned in order that he might gain a transfer to some less secure place than a convict establishment. Having friends in Liverpool, he was sent to Rainhill. where he arrived a compare- ' tively short time ago. He was duly visited by his friends, aud about a fortnight ago a somewhat large deputation of male friends went over from the city to Rainhill to see the patient, who, needless to say, was showing that the change of air and Ecene had been of great benefit to him. It is believed that at this inter- view a clever and complete plan of escape ..:as arranged. The patient was supplied with tools which enabled him Ito remove screws and nails and to cut out the window I of his cell.On the appointed night heapparently divested himself of all his clothing and went out by the window to a spot where evidently his friends were waiting with an entirely new outfit and a conveyance.A motor-car was ' probably used, for the escape was discovered in less than a quarter of an hour, and about fifty officers and attendants were at once turned out in search of the patient. The police of the 'whole neighbourhood were informed, but not the slightest trace of the escaped " lunatic " could be discovered. It will be reassuring to the public to learn, that the man who sensationally escaped from Rainhill Asylum has been rearrested. He is now under remand on a burglary charge. He is twenty-eight years of age, and was reeently brought up at the police court, Dale- street, on a charge of burglary, he having been found, it is said, in a house in Hartington-road, which he had burglariously entered. He was remanded till Friday morning.

STRANGE AFFAIR AT WIDNES.

On Monday night Peter Finnerty, of 8, Bedford- street, Widnes, was found by a boy lying in an uncon- scious state in a field in the vicinity of Moss Bank. He was taken to the Accident Hospital, and during the night he regained consciousness. It appears that on Monday afternoon this man and two others had a dis- pute about a race, and the man alleges that after he had struck one of the others he wns violently struck and knocked to the ground, where he injured his head, and it is supposed that he was carried into the field and left there. The affair caused quite a sensation in the neighbourhood. Upon inquiry at the hospital, Nurse Nutter said that he was making satisfactory progress.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l thank you very much for the Penknife 1 received, and now I am going to try for the Competition, entitled BEST AUTOBIOGRAPHY OF A PENKNIFE. I first saw daylight at the Sheffield Cutlery Works, from whence I was placed in a shop, and there remained for three whole months, until a little boy came, and, addressing the shopman, said "Please, how much is that lit- tle knife in the middle of the window?" The shopman replied one shilling; much was my delight when the boy had purchased me. I was just beginning to start on my travels. When the boy got home-he found,. much to his sorrow, that he had lost me. . . . Mean- while I was lying in the gutter, where I was picked up by an old man, and he used me to cut up twist tobacco. I was beginn,ing to get sorrowful, but, to my joy, he left me on the table, where his grandson, John, picked me up, and took me to one of his playmates, whom 'he asked to buy me for 3d. I thought this was a bargain, but the other boy refused to buy me. Now this grandson, John, vast a sailor, and he told his grandfather that he was to go to sea, and he told him, waulf.l find me (the 'knife) very useful. When the day came he set off for Liverpool, ' and took me -on board a great ship named "The Minorca," but one day he lent me to a fellow sailor, who wanted me to cut some slits of woad to place in a tree pot. He was leaning over the railing on deck, cutting away at the wood, when he accidentally dropped me into the sea, where I shall always re- main. From your loving nepheW).. , ERNST HARROLD.

GOD'S LANTERNS.

When day is gone and shadows ei•eap, And one by kn e the clear stars peep. I watch them as they shine so bri.3ht, When all around is dark as night. And then, I, think, God must have seen; How very.dark it would have been If there tid been no light to show 1 The proper way man had to go. For they would • often lose their way, If no suni shone as ` n the day; They would be full of dread and• fear, In all the 'dark With no one near. ' And so He puts his lanterns out That men might see their way about, ,And weary travellers who roam, ' night find through Him the right way home. Your loving ,niece, DAISY BAKEWELL. A merry little whistler Goes by my door each day; He whistles at his work, and He whiStles at his play.

The British Empire Championships. On the 30th ult. at Belfast the British Empire Championships were decided, the distances being 1 mile, 1 mile, and 5 miles respectively. Dan Flynn of Scotland secured the quarter by a length from Ernest Payne (England), Ben Jones (England) being third. In the mile Payne was not to be denied, and England's mile champion romped in a winner in front of Jones. All the heats of the mile were won by England except the fifth, secured by Flynn. The latter, however, got beaten in the semi-finals, in which H. D. Buck also was put out by Payne. Buck, however, won the 5 miles by a length from Jones. Flynn, the quarter-mile chain; pion, swept the board this season in the Scottish championship, which proves his exceptional supe- riority. Last year, it will be remembered, the Empire contests took place at Glasgow, H. D. Buck winning the quarter, and J. S. Benyon annexing the mile. Buck's form has temporarily declined, and, of course, Benyon is now a professionaL Ireland's riders were outclassed this year. Attempts at Record. This is the most propitious time of all the year for long distance riding, and there are reported to be several crack riders in severe training just now for attempts at record. More than one failure to shift the figures of the big endurance rides has attended recent attempts, but more successful results will probably be reported ere long. Meanwhile, those who are invited to help in these attempts cannot be too careful in thoroughly, mastering the rules of the game. In an attempt at record on the road, followers must not approach the rider closer than 100 yards while he is in the saddle. Keep

119 and when the rider stops it should be re- membered that the competitor's bicycle must not be wheeled a single hundred yards. He must ride all the way. Perhaps some of my readers may be asked to assist

The Feeding Arrangements

of an attempt at record. Remember that it is prohibited to ride alongside the competitor for the purpose of handing him food or drink. Station yourself on rising ground, where the competitor 'will naturally be going rather slowly:as he passes you. _

As the competitor approaches, signal hint by holding the food in the right hand extended towards him, and run a few paces alongside him as he takes it from you, so as to make sure he gets it safely.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
The Infirmary Debate.

Sir,—Your report, in the" Examiner" of last Satur- day of the debate in the Town Council. on the question of a donation to the Infirmary Building Fund, was interesting reading. The discussion was, to be sure, a little breezy, after the manner of the Council but, happily in this case, the wind was not boisterous. The Councillors, however, did not come to any definite decision in the matter but, like a Scotch Sheriff in a difficult case, they took it to avizandum. Suggestions for pntting the Infirmary upon a better footing than it is at present might be made, but we defer making them until we have the Council's decision before us. In the 'meantime it can do no harm to point out the precarious and unsatisfactory ways in which funds for that institu- tion are scraped together at present. Spasmodic cheques upon the unearned increment by rich men; perfunctory contributions of a penny a week from working men earning a pound a week and under; the free proceeds of assemblies for music and dancing, and collections derived from shows on the streets, seem to be rather ignobleJand uncertain methods of providing housing and relief for suffering humanity, mostly of the htunblet kinds. One pleasing circumstance connected with the 'management of the Infirmary, however, is worthy of letters of gold. I refer to the truly generous conduct of the local medical men in rendering their valuable services without money and without price. lON A.

Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival. Sir,—The forthcoming demonstration promises to excel in every respect the parade of four years ago, and with the hearty co-operation of workers and spectators alike the promoters are sanguine that their increasing laborers of the last few weeks will be highly successful. It must, however, be clearly pointed out that the space of time when collections can be taken en route is very limited, and the task of soliciting aid from every one of the forty or fifty thousand spectators is no light one. The committee therefore respectfully suggest that ladies and gentlemen whose windows and premises along the route are occupied by their friends should constitute themselves collectors within their own circle. This would immensely assist the movement and all sums so realized would be gladly acknowledged by the Hon. Treasurer (Ar. C. Leigh, Bewsey Chambers) or the Hon. Secretaries. It would also greatly add to the beauty of the demonstration if those ?shopkeepers and householders on the route of the procession where it lies in their power, would decorate their premises by making a display of bunting &c. As some misunderstanding seems to have arisen as to where vehicles are to join the procession, and also as to where ' collectors can obtain boxes, &c., it may be mentioned that vehicles will assemble in Bath-street Via Legh- street not later than 4.30, and that boxes and bags will be given out at Bank House on Friday from 7.0 to 9.0 p.m. and on Saturday from 1.0 p.m. The committee are desirous that the public shall not congregate in Bath-street to view the procession, as the work of marshalling would thereby be made exceedingly difficult. Thanking you for the prominence; you have given to the above movement, we remain on behalf of the Committee yours truly, W. H. BELLIAN, Chairman. E. PICKAVANCE, Hon Sec. E. A. SANDERS, Assistant Hon. Sec.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Church Commission Report.

Sir,—As one who has been before the above, I differ from some of their conclusions and remedies suggested. The latter, considering the lawlessness of the" all-round traitors " is not half " drastic " enough, men who teach also practice aaricular confession, prayers for the dead, adopt the altar-ward position in the Lord's Supper, burn candles when not required for giving light, described in Article 35 " as the act of a madman," wear Popish Mass Vestments, looked upon by Clement of Alexandria, A.D. 102, "as only fit for the tomfooleries of the priests of Bacchus," who wear the Mitre or the foolscap, alias the successor of Dagen the fish god, see Hislop's Two Babylons published by Partridge and Co. Men who circulate out and out Romish Manuals should be dispensed with at once ; to an honest mind they are obtaining money under false pretences. No merchant or even Army or Navy would tolerate men of such principles. During twenty years the Romanising practises have advanced by leaps and bounds, for example the so ciiled Altar-ward position was practised in 1662 churches, now it is in over 7000. Candle burning, when not required for giving light, from 581 churches to 4765. Incense burning (a mixture of about a dozen ingredients, whereas the book of Exodus gives four only) from nine churches to about 400. Vestments from 336 to 2158, a relic of the priests of Baal. Again the majority of the bishops and clergy have set their farms egaiest the Scriptural Eveuln7.. Communion. 'Why? So that their dupes may come to the unscriptural so called celebration in the morning fasting. No wonder the laity are disgusted, driven into Nonconformity or fail altogether in attending any place of worshiy,and who is to blame ? The unfaithful bishops.—Yours truly, VERITY. July 9th, 1006.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
.Trade Unionists and "Scabs."

have been requested to forward the following resolution moved by a member and passed at our last meeting :—" That the members of the Albion Lodge of thej Iron and Steel Workers' Association have no sympathy with the scathing remarks made by some members of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council at their last meeting in referring to the unemployed Non-unionists engaged by the Corporation in painting the market ac scabs &c., as reported in the local press. We agree that the Town Council adopted the right course in trying to alleviate the prevailing distress by directly employing and distributing the work among the unemployed instead of letting it out to contractors. We also holdthe view that it is the duty of all trade unionists to holdout a helping hand to our unfortunate brethren and endeavour to win them to our opinion tbat it is only by united action that the worker can obtain a just recompense for his labour. We sympathathise with these men who have bad the misfortune to be thrown out of work in many cases through no fault of their own and who are outside the trade unions through their inability to pay their contributions owing to lack of employment. The action of the Trades Council in this matter appeals to us the more strongly seeing that it is only a few weeks since they were arrogating to them- selves the sole right to form an out of work committee to enforce upon the Government and Corporation the necessity of finding work for all who were unemployed. It appears to us ridiculous that the Council who such a short time since were advocating the bounden duty of the Corporation to see that work was provided for all should now limit its operation to the members of Trades Unions only and leave their lees fortunate brethren to die of starvation."—Thanking you in Anticipation, yours &c.,

JOSEPH ELWELL. '

(Our correspondent has clearly "mistaken the shadow for the substance "this week,notwithstanding the warning we gave him last week. We can find nothing in the letter above which is at all pertinent to the subiect at issue—votes for women, or "the savage suffragettes." Instead, Mr. Osborne falls foul of the Editor, which is always an unwise thing to do seeing that he has the privilege of the last word. We are still friendly to the cause of" Votes for Women "and we still think the " savage suffragette" damages that cause by her ill-considered and unwomanly refusal to behave like a citizen and obey the law. As to what constitutes argument, our correspondent must be a very poor judge or he would never have willingly forsaken the field of argument for that of invective. Mistaking "the shadow for the substance" is by no means a catch- phrase but a very full and complete description of the case in point. The discussion must now cease as there is nothing to he gained by prolonging it, and on our corres- norulent's side it shows unmistakable signs of having become threadhare.—En. " Ex.'l

EARLESTONVN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY'S GALA. ne fourth annual gala in connectinn with the Earles- town Industrial Co-operative Society was held on Saturday, on the society's field adjoining the Orange. The weather was most favourable, and amid the beauti- ful surrounding, 2,00 children and 1,100 adults spent a delightful time. The children and others were marshalled into procession on the Market-square at lhalf-pest one and paraded to the field to the strains of the Viaduct Prize Brass Band. The committee of which Mr. Albert Onions is secretary,had made capital arrange- ments, and the afternoon proceedings included not only selections by the band, but entertainments by Professor's Royal Punch and Judy, and Professor F. Mordannt (ventriloquist), whilst sports were zealously entered by the children. The latter were ably managed by Mr. S. Schofield and Mr. NV. H. Teece. Free refreshments were served to the children, that department being under the management of Mr. J. H. Birks. The grounds adjoining the field were thrown open for inspection to the adults. Dancing was subsequently indulged in, the band plo.y log in a manner that afforded every satisfac- tion.

THE CYCLE PARADE. LATEST ARRANGEMENTS.

The fancy dress cycle parade to be held on the 21st inst in aid of the fund for the extension of the War- rington Infirmary, promises to be an even greater success than the two parades which have preceded it in recent years. The organizing work has been taken up with the greatest enthusiasm and the work of arranging the preliminary details is now almost complete. The general committee met at the Patten Arms Hotel on Friday night. Mr. W. H. Tiernan occupied the chair. The correspondence included an acknowledgment from the family of the late Alderman John Roberts of the vote of condolence passed by the committee, and also a letter frorneMr. Frank Webster in reply to the letter of thanks from the committee to the Warringten District of Independent Order of Oddfellows, M.U., for their postponethent of the picnic arranged by them from Saturday, July 21st, to Saturday, August 18th. The SECRETARY announced the engagement of the Annamese Straw Dancers for the procession, and also that the Working Men's Committee of the Infirmary had promised to get as many collectors as possible. He further stated that the Volunteer Band had consented to take part in the procession. As an evidence of the outside interest in the event, Mr. Pickavance said inquiries regarding it had come from various parts of Lancashire, Cheshire and Derbyshire, and also from Wrexham. .

Reports from the various sub-committees showed that the work of preparation was nom well advanced, and Mr. E. Morris, on behalf of the Marshals' Com- mittee, gave the order of procession, which showed that it would be one of the best ever seen in Warrington. . .

Mr. BprKs said that many of the inhabitants of the Bank Quay district were disappointed that the pro- cession was not to go round that part of the town, but it was stated in reply that the arrangements could not now be altered. A great objection to visiting the district was the two bridges which had to be crossed. A meeting of ladies willing to assist in the above object was held on Monday evening in the Town Hall. Mrs. Pickmere • had promised to take the chair, but finding it impossible to be present, she found an admirable and eflicient substitute in Mrs. W. Fletcher, of Whitley. About 50 or 60 ladies were present including Miss Burgess (Wilderspool), and Miss Watkins (matron Warrington Infirmary.) The Parade Committee Was represented by Messrs. W. H. Bellian (chairman), G. Bowman (vice-chairman), G. H. Smith, J. H. J.flampson, E. Pickavance (general hon. secretary), and E. A. Sanders (assistant hon. secretary) Mr. Bellian introduced Mrs. Fletcher,who, in a neat and appropriate speech, explained the objects of the meet- in.., which were to secure lady collectors for the day of parade, to look after the stand boxes in the streets, and to take up the book collection. Various ladies offered their services, and upwards of 20 collect- ing books were taken out. On the proposition of Mr. W. H. BELLIAN, seconded by Mr. HAMPSON, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Fletcher for presiding. Mrs. FLETCHE& briefly responded, and appealed to all the ladies present to do their utmost for the success of the parade. Ladies who could not attend the meeting are reminded that collecting books can be obtained on application to Mr. E. Pickavance,67, Bridge-street, and that a prize, value ,t 2 29., will be awarded to the Lidy with the largest book collection. Those taking part in the parade will assemble in the park and a start will be made from the Town Hall at five o'clock. Proceeding along Sankey-street the I following route will then be followed. Buttermarket- I street, Church-street, round the lamp opposite the Parish Church, Church-street, Mersey-street. Horse- market-street, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane, Crostield- ' street, Parker-street, Wilson-Patten-street, round the canons, Bridge-street, Rylands-street and Palmyra- square to the Parr Hall. As has been previously announced peirrot performances will be given opposite Miss Garven's house, Sankey-street. from three o'clock until the procession starts, when the artistes will proceed to the canons where the entertainment will be continued until the procession reaches that point.

-HEAD TEACHERS' HALF-YEARLY REPOtiTS.

......- --..-..- The half-yearly reports of the Head Teachers upon the assistant teachers in the schools in the borough Nvere received through the managers,and it was resolved that the same be approved.—A letter was submitted from the Honorary Secretary of the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Teachers, asking the Com- mittee to receive a deputation in reference to the half- yearly reports upon the assistant teachers, and it was decided to receive the deputation at the September meeting of the Committee.

TECHN— ICAL SCHOOL AND CONTEs:CATION SCHOOL ARRANGEMENTS.

In reference to the Central Technical school (hitherto known as the Technical Institute), it has been decided that the teachers and salaries of the occasional staff should boss last session, any additions thereto or alterations therein that may be found necessary to be submitted td the committee. It was also decided to offer those appointments to the teachers who held the positions last session. With reference to the branch technical schools (hitherto called Evening Con- tinuation schools) it was resolved (a) that the schools be opened on three evenings each week; (b) that the salaries of the Superintendents be at the rate of 10s. Gd. per evening, and those of the assistants at the rate of (is. per evening when giving instruc- tion to the students, and that teachera of special subjects (metalwork, domestic economy, technical drawing, and commercial arithmetic and correspondence) be appointed, if required, at a salary at the rate of 7s. (id. per evening ; (c) that the superintendents and assistant teachers be required to do the requisite cor- recting and marking of papers, returns of attendance, etc.: (d) that the following be offered appointments as superintendents, viz. : Fairfield (Girls), Miss Mary Taylor ',• Hamilton-street (Boys), Mr. Frank H. Longshaw ;

_ Heathside (Boys), M. Thomas Edden ; Warrington British (Mixed), Mr. Sydney W. Jeffery; Wycliffe British (Girls), Miss Leah Clay ; and (e) that the assistant teachers be appointed on the recommendation of the superintendents. THE BEANIONT SCHOOL.—LABOURERS' RATE OF PAY

At a meeting of the Sites and Buildings Committee on July 6th, the Secretary of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council (Mr. Councillor Fred Stott) had an interview with the Committee in reference to the rate of payment by the Contractor for the erection of the " Beamont " 'Council • school to certain labourer employed.—After some discussion, it was decided to hold a special meeting of the Committee at the school at three o'clock on the afternoon of Wendesday, the 11th July, for the purpose of hosting the views of the Contractor upon the matter. A NEW INFANTS' SCHOOL REQUIRED.

Upon the recommendation of the sub-committee appointed to consider the suggested alterations to the premises of the Trinity National Infants' school, it was resolved, (a) that imperative instructions be ;riven for a site for a new school to be obtained in Welltleld-street immediately ; (b) that steps be taken for the provision of a senior and infants' school in iron buildings, which could be removed to any other site when required ; (c) that the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee be approached with a view to the public playground in Welllleld-street being used as the playground of the school ; and that, in the event of such permission being obtained, the sanction of the Board of Education to the arrangement be asked; and (d) that the temporary iron buildings be erected in the playground or on the site for the Proposed school.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington's return to League cricket on Saturday I was marked by a welcome victory over Stanley. Earles- town still retain their position at the head of the I League but Warrington are only a single point behind. The large attendance at Saturday's match testified to the increasad interest which is being taken in the summer sport, and as to whether it is maintained or not largely depends on the form which theclub displays. There was no brilliant scoring on either side and the game was not particularly exciting. Stanley went to the wicket first but cut a sorry figure against the deliveries of E. A. Connor and Cresswell, the innings closing for 52. Some idea of the lowness of individual scores is gained from the fact that the best display was that of R. W. Killip who knocked up twelve. E. A. Connor and Cresswell did particularly effective work with the ball, tthe first named capturing 15 wickets for 28 runs while the professional was responsible for 4 wickets for 17 runs. The home fielding was also very smart.

Warrington were in a better scoring mood, and their combined efforts produced 97 runs. The most success- ful batsman was J. W. Bibby, who ran up eighteen. Features of the game were the valuable innings of %V. Richardson and T. Wright, who made 16 and 15 respec- tively. Alsop and Moore obtained good bowling analyses, their respective captures being 5 wickets for 28 runs, and 3 wickets for 34.

The second eleven gave a splendid account of them- selves at Stanley. Having made 198 for the loss of six wickets they declared. R. H. Carson gave a dashing display, his 44 including six fours. R. G. Pierooint was also in first rate form, and compiled 23. Stanley were all dismissed for 106, Bennett taking three wickets for 33. Thewlis four for 40, and Dolan one for none. Carson did good service behind the stumps.

St. James's gained a victory over Sankey Wire Mills 2nd, Tole (21) and Minards (14) batting very Well.

Milling's employees disposed of the Secondary School in ridiculously easy fashion. They ran up 69 for thet loss of only two wickets and dismissed their opponents for 67. Pickersgill was to the fore with the bat,knock- ing up 36 not out.

There was an exceedingly close struggle between Penketh Friends and St. Paul's, the latter securing the victory by one rim. Proud (19) for the Friends made the best individual score of the match.

Penketh defeated Garston 2nd by a majority of 20, Dumbell (23) giving a creditable exhibition with the bat.

In the second round of the Works Competition on Tuesday evening Whitecross rather easily accounted for Firth Company. Batting first they put together SG for the loss of six wickets, Duckworth (23), Brierley (15), and Hackney (16), all playing well for their runs. The Firth fared badly after the dismissal of Wright, who hit 25 in the course of five minutes when he was out to a fine catch at third man.

On Thursday the Warrington eleven entertained Wigan and trounced them in no half-hearted fashion. The visitors were all dismissed for 73 to which War- rington responded with 241 with only nine wickets down. Podmore bowled with deadly effect, capturing six wickets for eight runs.

Bowling. The second rovid in connection,with the Warrington Works Competition will be concluded to-day and the afternoon's programme should witness some of the most interesting matches of the competiton. Rylands (No. 1) and Whitecross (No. 1) are evenly matched, though the probability is that the last-named team will retain their place in the competition. Richmonds' should secure a victory at the expense of Penketh Tannery and in all probability a few points only should separate Sankey Wire Mills and Pearson and Knowles (No. 3). Pearson and Knowles (No. 1) will experience little difficulty in disposing Robinsons'.

Perhaps the most interesting of last Saturday's games was the one between Whitecross (No. 2) and Fletcher Russell (No. 2). Whitecroqa made a bad start the first game going against them 15-1. A win 15-6 improved matters slightly but an immediate defeat by a similar margin left the game unchanged. Two games later they had fallen still further in arrears, the totals stand- ing at 4,3-53. D. Davies put up a good fight against J. Horgan and after peeling at 4 and again at 10, 11 and 14, he was only beaten by the odd point. It was left to W. Ball to turn the tide and after his defeat of Henderson by 15-8, Whitecross never looked back and the score was from 72-81 to 102-93 and finally to 140-122.

L. and N.-W. led throughout in their match with Rylands (No. 2), though at one stage their position was seriously challenged. Starting well they went to the front 30-9, but the balance of the remaining points went to Rylands, who with eight men off the green had crept to within three points ot the Railway men's score. Much ground was however lost by Edelsten and Shel- don, and though R. Savory made a strong effort to retrieve their position they were defeated 149-123.

C.L.C. never once looked like winning in their game with Fletcher's (No. I).True they made a good fight,but losing the lead at the start their position steadily be- came worse,and in the end 25 points separated the teams. Rylands (No. 4) had also the fate to see an opening lead by their opponents (Crosfield's No. 1) gradually increased, and had to admit defeat by 175 points to 200.

The draw for the third round and also for the con- solation competition takes place on Monday evening, and matches in both events will commence the follow- ing Saturday.

Whilst defeated by a handsome margin on Saturday, ' the Saracen's Head team beat Winnington by 42 on the home and home l matches. The local team's display on 'Saturday was truly inglorious only three games being won out of 16 played.

The pericdical mains at the Liberal Club continue to attract attention and on Saturday an enjoyable after- noon's contest ended as follows :-I,J. Sankey ;2, W.H. Taylor: 3, W. EL Dutton, jun.; 4, C. Jordan Taylor.

WARRINGTON GROCERS' ASSOCIATION.

A Protest Regarding the Compensation Question. The annual meeting of the members of the Warring- ton and District Grocers'and Provision llealers'Associa- tion as held in the British kVorkmin Cocoa Rooms, Horsemarket-street, on Wednesday. There was only a small attendance.

TIIE REPORT. The committee in their annuli report said:—The ques- tion of almanacs and Christmas boxes came up for con- sid9ration daring the year and we are pleased to report that although the giving of these was not altogether abolished a very considerable advance was made towards its total abolition. We regret that the attempt to fix a minimum selling price for sugar (wholesale) which had worked well for a considerable period has broken down, and hope the members will co-operate to obtain a fair margin of profit. We note with satisfaction the appointment of a select committee by the President of the Board of Agriculture to consider the Pure Butter Bill. The fore- cast of their recommendations which appears in the press would appear to justify their proceeding. We note that they recommend the inspection of all butter factories as in the case of margarine, the prohibition on the premises of substances used in the adulteration of butter, the fixing of a pure butter standard of 16 per cent, of water, and refuse to recommend any special exemption for Irish dairy butter. All these proposals are viewed with favour by your committee and we ask the Association to do all it can to strengthen the hands of the Minister of Agriculture in his efforts to intro- dues and pass a genuine Pure Butter Bill. The report was approved. Mr. Povev announced that the funds of the Associa- tion had increased during the year by Ll,l and there was now left a balance in hand of about £4O. ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

Mr. W. Monks was elected president and Messrs. Gandy, Kay and Redding were elected vice-presidents. Mr. .1. P. Gorst resigned the secretaryship and was succeeded by Mr. W. Owen. Messrs. Bowdon, Taylor, Gorst, Dockworth,Ric ardson and Gerrard were elected to form the committee Mr.T.S.Steel was re-appointed solicitor to the Associ tion.

- ASSOCIATION AND THE COMPENSATION ACT. • • . • •

The.Xs's-o-c-itiri.in:oereeded to consider the provision of the Workmen's Compensation Act Amendment Act as it affected shop keepets who employed three or MOTE assistants. It was resolved "That in the opinion of this meeting the definition of a 'workman 'as provided by the new amendments, is likely to prove detrimental to the happy relationships existing between employers and shop assistants, is not called for under the circum- stances of the case, and should not be passed by Parlia- ment until a full enquiry has been made into the question whether the necessity exists." 10 a letter it ' was decided to send to Mr. A. H. Croslield, M.P., Mr. A. J. King, M P., Mr. W. Crossley, M.P. and Mr J. A. Seddon, M.P.,it was pointed out that the interests of the Association which represented a large number of retail traders, would be most serionsly affected by the provision. They would readily understand, particularly in the case of a very small shopkeeper, such a statutory obligation might mean total ruin. Indeed such a law if made could never be carried out, and the small employer would doubtless prefer to go bankrupt rather than face the burden of insurance. In conclusion, they point out that the work in a retail grocer's shop is of a light and unhazardons description, and call upon the four gentlemen to offer their strongest opposition "to the proposal until an enquiry has shown that necessity exists for such drastic provisions.
