The Examiner Saturday, July 14, 1906. WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS. INDUSTRIAL CHEMISTS AT WARRINGTON. PRESENTATION OF MEDALS AND Visit to Messrs. Crosfields' Works. CANES. I Thursday was a day to be long remembered in the history of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons. Ltd., March to Moore. Warnugton, for the firm was honoured with a visit ' from a numerous assembly of members of the Society of Chemical Industry and their friends. This organization is the largest technical society in the world, and embraces all English-speaking lands, the membership being over 4,000. During the week the annual meetings of the Society have been held in Manchester, and Thursday was set apart for visits to places of particular interest. Those who availed themselves of the opportunity' of inspecting Messrs. Crosfield's wwks numbered about three hundred. They ,arrived at the Central Station at 11.40, and immediately proceeded in conveyances to the works. The party included Dr. Edward Divers, F.R.S., London (president of the Society), hr. Eustace Carey, Liver- pool (president-elect), Professor W. J. Pope, Mr. Samuel Hall (hon. treasurer), Mr. Oscar Guttatann, Mr. Watson Smith, Mr. J. Hfibner (hon. local secre- tary), and Mr. Chas. G. Cresswell (secretary). On reaching the works the visitors at once entered the handsome general offices and were divided into parties, each in charge of a guide, to be conducted through the various departments. Amongst those in waiting to receive the company were Mr. J. J. Orosfield, Captain G. R. Crosfield, Dr. Markel; Mr. H. Kingdon, Mr. H. Roberts and other heads of the firm. Needless to say, the visit was full of interest to those who had not previously had the opportunity of seeing Messrs. Crosfield's manufactures in actual progress, and all appeared much impressed with the up-to-date pro- cesses.and the splendid conditions under which they are carried out. Messrs. Crosfield, of course, enjoy a high reputation for the social side of their work, everything possible being done to brighten the sur- roundings of the employees—who number 2,ooo—and to make them take a real interest in the tasks allotted to them.

Warrington people are justly proud of the local Volunteer battalion and considerable interest was taken in the parade and route march to Moore Hall which took place on Saturday. The weather was ideal and large crowds witnessed the assembly of the battalion on the lawn facing the Town Hall. The infantry wore new khaki uniforms and the other volunteers taking part in the proceedings were the cyclists' company' mounted infantry company, machine gun section trans- port section, signallers and stretcher bearers, and the 'regimental bands, the total numbering 726. The officers in attendance iucluded Brigadier-Colonel Verschoyle,Lieut.-Colonel R. Carruthers(Brigade Major) Lient.-Colonel Edelsten, Major B. Fairclough, Major 4. D. Fairclongh, Major Appleton, Captains Layton,Owen, Williamson, Smith, Crosfield, and Avery, Lieutenants Ridgway, Picton. Bournphrey, Roberts, Monks, Woods, and Fairclough, Surgeon-Lieut. Collins, Acting-Chaplain Rev. W. Bracecamp, Major and Adjutant A. F. Bundock, and Sergeant-Major Ingram. Before the departure for Moore there was an inter- esting ceremony—the presentation of long service and good conduct medals to Colour-Sergeant-Instructor Cleaver (permanent staff), Colour-Sergt. C. Russell, and Private Higgins ; and of Colonel Edelsten's recognition of the conduct of the following tent-pitching team in the Liverpool Military Tournament of last March : Sergeant Connor, Lance-Corporal Hill, Privates Heath, Millet, Melling, Conroy, awl G. Knight. To each mem- ber of the team Colonel Edelsten presented a handsome silver-mounted Malacca cane, suitably inscribsd. Colonel EDELSTEN, addressing the battalion, said that before they commenced the march there was one very pleasant duty to perform. That was the presenta- tion of three medals for long service and good conduct, as well as a slight award to the seven members of the tent-pitching team, who competed at the Liverpool tournament. With regard to the latter, most of them were aware that in the competition they won their heat "hands down with plenty to spare," but, owing to something they did not quite understand, they were not allowed to compete again. The system of h)ats was done away with, and they had to abide by the decision. They did not dispute that decision, but at the same time they thought it was a little hard. He was, there- fore, going to give each member of the team a cane' or " swagger " stick, as a recognition on behalf of the battalion, of their excellent conduct in the hall, and of the extraordinary care they had taken to be worthy of the battalion.

The imposing and commodious offices first of all came in for a great deal of admiration. Features which attracted particular attention were the fine stained glass dome and the fibrous plaster shields representing various nations, all having been cleverly executed by Mr. Hiller. Next the main chemical laboratory, which occupies the block of buildings formerly used as general offices, was inspected and was seen to be fitted with the best systems of testing raw materials and chemical products. A great deal of research work is carried on and there are Ilvesmaller laboratories devoted to special processes. Passing on to the lithographic and letterpress department the parties viewed the 'many modern appliances, and they were next conducted to the soap warehouse which has a storage capacity of over 1,000 tons. The household soap department presents a strikingly busy scene and the visitors spent some time I iniwatching the stamping of tablets bySpecial machines one of which stamps no fewer than 40 tons per day. The tablets are also wrapped by machinery with great rapidity. One of the most interesting departments to the average onlooker is that where all the stages of work incidental to the production of toilet specialities are in full swing. The chief processes are the combined ones of" milling "and " plodding " in which the purest soap obtainable is reduced to shreds, and after being passed between the massive granite rollers of the mills, I and mixed with specially blended perfumes, is passed Ithrough the " plodding" machines to issue from these in the form of bars. These bars are afterwards cut and stamped into tablets, which are then wrapped and packed. In the latter work a large number of girls are engaged and display great dexterity. They are encour- aged to relieve the regular routine by singing and it i was pleasing to'hear the familiar strains of "Killarney." I , Space forbids detailed mention of the many- sided operations carried on in this. great hive of industry. To most of the visitors it was really a I revelation to find what an extensive range the manufac- tures embraced. There is a wood box department in which boxes are produced at the rate of 20,000 to 40,000 a week; a soap-boiling department with a pan of 160 tons capacity and others of 109 tons ; glycerine works with vacuum pumps which evaporate 300 tons of water per day ; tin box making and engraving sections, and an engineering workshop which has turned out locomotives, engines for steam boats, and a complete vacuum evapor- ating plant with a heatine surface of 4,000 square feet. The parties also witnessed the packing of carbosil in bags—made by machinery in another part of the works —and also wen* to the riverside warehouse (10,000 tons capacity) from which they had good view of the tower of the new Transporter bridge which is being constructed across the Mersey to communicate with the works for the manufacture of cement and paints now in course of erection. The concluding stage of the tour was the silicate department in whictiwater glass is manufac- tured with the aid of furnaces heated to 2912 degrees. The firm are the largest makers of water glass and chemically pure glycerine in the world. The inspection of the works afforded convincing evidence of the growth of the Beautiful Warrington movement. Here and there were to be seen window boxes and hanging baskets of pretty flowers and plants, and the effect was very pleasing. At 1.30 luncheon was served in the Workmen's Dining Hall which was gaily decorated. The works orchestra, under the conductorship of Mr. F. H. Crossley, was in attendance and played a choice select on of music. The catering was admirably carried out by the Warrington Cafii Company. Owing to the length of the day's pro- gramme the directors had requested that no speeches should be made. Dr. DIVERS, however, asked the members of the Society and friends to support him in passing a hearty vote of thanks to Messrs. Crosfield for their great ' hospitality and for the thorough way in which they hal I opened up to the visitors' inspection the marvellous works they had constructed and all the accessories that led to the success of the works. (Applause). The vote was accorded with the greatest enthusiasm. Dr. Martim, while expressing surprise at this ex- traordinary "breach of discipline," confessed that under the circumstances he forgave Dr. Divers with pleasure, because it gave him (Dr. Markel) an opportunity of welcoming the guests most heartily on behalf of his co-directors and the staff. If at the end of the day the visitors felt that they had not been disappointed it would be a great pleasure to the firm for which they , thanked them most hearity. (Applause.) I Immediately after luncheon an exceedingly smart turn out of the Fire Brigade called forth warm praise. The alarm was sounded and in the remarkably short time of three quarters of a miuute the small fire engine had been got to work. It was followed a minute later by the large horse engine and water was soon being ejected at the rate of 750 gallons a minute. One jet—said to be the highest in Lancashire—throws the water to a height of 150 feet. The engines are con- stantly kept under steam pressure so as to be ready in case of any emergency. Superin- tendent Gallon ably directed the demonstration. The ambulance corps gave a creditable exhibition of "first aid " and the Works Volunteer Company went through a series of interesting evolutions. During the afternoon an enjoyable concert was given in the Works Assembly Room by the Works Choir and Orchestra, and there was a gymnastic display by girls. The Works Brass Band, conducted by Mr. Halliwell, also gave selections. At 3.30 tea was served in the roof garden and the girls dining room, and the visitors left to catch the 4.53 train, all being highly delighted with the events of the day.

The interesting ceremony over, Colonel EDELSTEN remarked that they were honoured that afternoon by the presence of Colonel Verschoyle, who was the officer recently appointed to command the South Lancashire Brigade, to which they had always belonged, but with which they had not drilled up to now. He supposed, however, that they would have the honour and pleasure of drilling with them after the next camp. He had pleasure in asking the Brigadier to present the three medals to those who had earned them. Colonel VERSCHOYLE then presented the three medals addressing a few appropriate words to each recipient, and warmly shaking hands with Colour-Sergeant- Instructor Cleaver.

The battalion immediately afterwards commenced their route march to Moore Hall Park, where exercises and battalion drill, &c. were gone through. For the pur- poses of the parade and route march it was supposed that Chester had been seized by a hostile force which had effected a landing in Wales) the advance troups of which had been seen on the high ground about Frodsham on Saturday morning. The mounted infantry patrolled the ground lying between the Ship Canal, and Warring- ton and Crewe rail way, the Warrington and Chester-road Keckewick and the Daresbury Delph, whilst the cyclists reconnoitred Halton, Sutton, and Dutton, the main body of the battalion being concentrated in the Park. The men afterwards formed up in bivouac order, and refreshments were served under the management of Quartermaster-Sergeant Willcocks. The battalion returned to the Drill Hall at about half-past nine.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDDING AT BEWSEY-ROAD CHURCH

Smith—Simpson.

A marriage which aroused considerable local interest was solemnized at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Tuesday afternoon, the contracting parties being Miss Eliza Simpson, second daughter of Mr. Charles Simpson, Lilac Villa, Bewsey-road, and Mr. Joseph Smith, son of the late Mr. Joseph Smith, of Garston. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. Joseph Manning (of Warrington) assisted by the Rev. William Howarth (of Gersten). Mr. W. Pearce presided at the organ and played appropriate selections including Mendelssohn's Wedding March." The bride was charmingly attired in a gown of ivory cream silk voile trimmed with lace and silk ruching with 'transparent yoke, and handsome embroidered tulle veil over a wreath of mini orange blossoms (the gift of Mr. and Mrs. Clarke). She carried a shower bouquet of white roses, lilies of the valley, smilax, and asparagus fern, and her ornaments included a gold locket with diamond setting, the gift of the bride- groom. She was attended by three bridesmaids— Miss Clara Atkinson wore a dress of rose coloured French voile trimmed with French lace, and a mush- room-shaped hat trimmed with pink roses and ivory chiffon. Her ornament was a gold brooch set with pearls, the gift of the bridegroom, and she carried a bouquet of sweet peas and smilax. Miss A. Winter- bottom and Miss Ivy Wilkinson were attired in white silk frocks, and their hair was adorned with bows of ribbon and marguerites. Their bouquets were also com- posed of marguerites and they wore gold brooches the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Isaac A. Simpson dis- charged the duties of best man, and Mr. Fred Simpson acted as groomsman. The wedding breakfast was served at the house of the bride's parent; and about 40 guests were present, including the Revs. J. Manning and W. Howarth, Mr. and Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Smith, Mr. and Mrs. Clarke (Ireland), Mr. and Mrs. B. Smith (Birming- ham). Later in the day the newly-married couple left for County Down, Ireland, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a brown costume trimmed with buff cloth, with hat to match. The following is a list of the presents : Bridegroom to Bride, silver-mounted dressing case and gold learet Mr. C. PROTECTING THE CHLDREN Annual Meeting of the Local N. S. P. C. C.

The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, was held at the Town Hall on IThursday afternoon. Mr. A. H. Lee presided, and it I WEIS to be regretted that there was only h small attend- ance. The committee in their annual report regret that there has been a large increase in the number of cases dealt with during the year. The total number of cases was 318, affecting 1,000 children, as against 278, the previous ' year. In 21 cases reconrce was had to the law and con- victions followed. 1903 visits of supervision were paid during the year. The committee report a successful I financial year the excess of income over expenses being I £lB us. The committee have however to call attention to the fact that the sum collected was £l3 75. Bd. less and expressed the hope that the ladies would ' do their utmost to maintain the income of the branch. The committee express their combined satisfaction with the work of Inspector Cox and also record their appre- ciation of the efforts of many public officials to assist the society's objects. in conclusion the committee desire to impressmpon everyone that the society's work is still only partly accomplished. " There still " they say " remain many wrongs against children which the society cannot at present reach. For instance it is a melancholy fact that in large towns owing either to the condition under which they are circumstanced, their . parents' ignorance,or improper method of feeding them about 50 per cent, of the children born into the world die before reaching their fifth year. The children are killed by neglect and bad feeding as surely as if they were actually done to death by violence. 111-fed,unfed, footsore and worn out and entirely uneducated there are hundreds of children whom the society is unable, owing to a deficiency in the law, to aid." The com- mittee express the hope that persons will report to the Inspector the movements of tramps who have children in their care.

The financial statement showed that a total sum of £2.10 16s. 11d. had been raised, including £129 ISs. in Warrington : 216 7s. 10 in Lymm ; and £l7 15s. Id. in Newton-le-Willows._

In answer to Mr. McGranon, Mr. W. D. JEANS, jun., said the subscriptions were upwards of £2O less than in the previous year. The reports were approved. ELECTION or OFFICERS. _ _

Mr. W. H. J. JENKINS moved the re-election of tha

president and vice-presidents, with the addition of the name of Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., to the list of vice- presidents. The general committee were also re-elected with the addition of the names of Alderman Forshaw, Captain Crosfield, Dr. Fox, Mr. James Dodds, Mr. C. E. rParker, Rev. G. C. Briggs' anti Mrs. Wilkinson, Alderman I Bennett, Miss Broadbent, Nfrs. George Dakin, Mr. V. B. Davies, Mrs. James Fairclougb, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. A. H. Lee. Mrs. Lung, Mr. A. Lister-Kaye, Mr. J. A. McGregor. Miss Massey,Mrs. Mauro, Miss Morris Mrs. H. Roberts, and Mrs. Wilkinson were elected to form the executive committee. An interesting address was then delivered by Mr. , SUMMERS, of the head office of the society. In their annual report a strong plea was made for the formation of a ministry for children. Events bad shown that the laws were not effectively administered and it was time that a special department for children should be formed to see that all measures affecting children were carried out. If they could only get the children well looked after and get the Act of Parliament put more generally into force many social problems of the present day would be partially if not completely solved. (Applause.) Mr. LEE paid a tribute of praise to the manner in which Inspector Cox carried out his work in Warring- ton, and moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Summers for his address.

Mr. J. F. AWBERY seconded, and suggested the appointment of lady inspectors. He regretted that the prison treatment at present was not punitive enough. Any man or woman who had shown inhumanity to chil- dren had lost all claim to human treatment, and a great deal of good would, he thought, be done if the prison treatment was made more harsh to those who ill-treated children. (Applause.)

The vote‘Tv'a; heartily accorded. Votes of thanks to the Nfayor for the use of the room and to Mr. Lee for presiding, concluded the meeting.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Services for Sunday.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church, preachers, 10.30, Rev. J. Manning ; 6.30, Rev. J. Harper (Widnes). Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, preachers, 10.30, Rev. S. Pitt; 6.30, Rev. J. Manning. Wycliffe Renovation Scheme.

A fairly well-attended meeting of the members and congregation of Wycliffe Church was held in the Memorial Buildings on Thursday evening. The Rev. John Yonge presided, and announced that two thirds of the amount required to carry out the scheme for the renovation of the church, had already been promised. It was unanimously decided to proceed with the work at once.

===== 0002601_19060714_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

At this Church on Sunday morning the Rev. J. Mann- ing preached, taking for his text the words : " Where- fore gird up the loins of yeur mind, be sober, and hope to the end for the grace that is to be brought unto you at the revelation of Jesus Christ "(L Peter 1., 13). At the evening service 'the • rostrum was occupied by the Rev. A. Elvidge, of Batley (where the Rev. S. Pitt was preaching the anniversary sermons) and he based a powerful discourse on the words: Abide with us ; for it is toward evening, and the day is far spent." -

Wesleyan Appointments.

The first draft of ministerial appointment in connec- tion with the Wesleyan Methodist Church has just been issued. It includes the following :—Warrington, Sidney Pitt, William Mellor (Padgate), Joseph Manning, Edward J. Gawne (Penketh), John Gunnell, Joseph Howard, supernumeraries ; Runcorn, T. Galland Hartley, Joseph Cartwright, Alfred Garland; Frodsham, William H. Mosley ; Widnes, William J. Baker, Frederick H. Bell, Harold R. Thacker ; St. Helens and Prescot, David Jones, Simon C. Hall (Earlestown, Newton-le. I Willows, Lancashire), Frederick V. Beaumont (Prescot), Robert S. Armsby, BA., J. Wesley Hetherington. Re-opening of St. Paul's Church. After having been closed for several weeks St. Paul's , Church was re-opened on Thursday night. The interior ' of the Church has been re-decorated and the building I now looks very bright and cheerful. There was a 1 large congregation at the service on Thursday evening, lat which the preacher was the Ven. Archdeacon I Spooner. The other clergy present were :—The Revs. W. Bracecamp and R. H. Bowen. Special anthems were sung by the choir, Mr. E. Wilkes presiding at the organ. The preacher's text was I. Kings, vi. 29, " And he carved on the walls of the house round about with carved figures and cheruhims and pains trees and open flowers."—A collection was taken on behalf of the renovation fund. Baptist Anniversary.

The anniversary services in connection with the Baptist Church, Latchford were held on Sunday, when there were large congregations. In the morning a young people's service was held, over which Mr. S. Albinson presided. An address was given by Mr. C. E. Parker, of Penketh, on "Sowing and reaping," and was much appreciated. The choir rendered the anthem" Let the people praise Thee," the soloist being Miss S. Lea. In the afternoon th-e preacher was the Rev. J. W. Varloy, of St. Armes., bottle-Sea, who took for his subject, "What mannerivjrchild shall this bo ?" Miss B. Richard son sang with great effect, "I know that my Redeemer liveth," while the choir were heard to great advantage in the anthem, " Call to remembrance," the solo being taken by Miss Eva Grounds. In the evening the Rev. J. W. Varley again occupied the pulpit. The antherd " Seek ye the Lord," was given by the choir, Mr. S, Hassell taking the solo. Miss B. Richardson afterwards sang with effect "Angels ever bright and fair." Special mention should be made of the choir and soloists, who acquitted themselves in an admirable manner. The collections, which were in aid of the church funds, totalled £l5 4s, Gd.

THE SALVATION ARMY.

Gift of 6 Villas by Mr. Henry Thornton, J P.

Mr. Henry Thornton, J.P., Warrington, has 're- sented to General Booth for the use of the Salvation Army six semi-detached villas, situatelin Victoria-road, Fearnhead, near to Mr. Thornton's owe private resi- dence. Vie are informed that the villas will be utilised as " Homes of Rest " by the workers of the Salvation Army, in order to enable them to recuperate after the strain of their labours. Mr. Thornton's gift is sure:to be much appreciated by the Salvation Army, and as Fearnhead bears a good reputation, from a health point of view, the villas can be well adapted for the purpose designed.

Warrington Boy Killed. Thomas Pritchard, (13), of Bostock-street, Warring- ton, emplo)ed by Thomas William Parker, botanical beer manufacturer, was instantaneously killed opposite Runcorn Market on Thursday. He was holding a horse attached to a loaded lurry when the animal started, threw up its head, sending the boy to the ground, and the wheels of the heavy lurry passed over the poor lad's neck. —The runaway was stopped a few hundred yards further on.

LOCAL NEWS. Great interest will be shown by all in the extremely interesting item of local news which we are able to print to-day. Such intelligence is welcome not only for itself, but because it will convey a grand .message of hope to all who read it. Mrs. E. Brown, 29, Clifton-street, Latchford, War- rington, says :—" Before I commenced to take Doan's backache kadney pills I was a great sufferer from inflam- mation of the kidneys. For a long time I was so bad that I didn't know what to do ; the pains in the small part of my hack were dreadful, and I was unable to do anything. I could not carry my baby, and it was a hard matter for me to get up after I had been bending. Sometimes the pain was a continual gnawing sensation, and my back seemed as if it would come in two. I tried all kinds of medicine, but none of it did me the least good. "It was different, however, when I commenced with Doan's kidney pills, for I soon found they were the right medicine for my ailment. I got easier after a. few doses and the continued use of the medicine brought me doses, to health. I can honestly say that Doan's pills are the only medicine that did me good. (Signed) E. Bnow:I." Doan's Tlackacbi Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence. Of all chemists and stores, or past free, direct from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London W. Do not forget to use the full name. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills.

The Campers' and Farmers' Union. The above is the title of a new association which should certainly prove useful to the growing number of cycle campers throughout the country during the holiday season. The idea of the Union is to further the mutual interests of farmers, land holders, and campers. • Members of the Union, from Galway to Kent, by mountain, sea, and river, are not only prepared to tolerate but to welcome the camper, giving him the use of beautiful estates in return merely for his custom as a consumer of his host's country produce during his stay in a particular locality. Full particulars of the Union's field of operations may be obtained f Mr. Arnold Eiloart, The Camp, Ditton Hill Farm, Surrey. A Brilliant Tricycle Ride. It was only quite recently that Mn Leonard Drake reduced the Brighton and back tricycle record, and now Mr. J. Dudley Dayinond, of the Bath Road Club, has succeeded in lopping another 5 min. off the figures for this hilly journey estab- lished by his iellow-elubman. Daymond's time being 6 hrs. 19 min. 48 see. It may be thought strange that attention should be devoted to the three wheeler as a speed machine, but nowadays tho safety records have reached so fine a point that very few riders can hope to achieve distinction by paring even seconds from existing figures. Hence the slight revival in favour of the tricycle amongst nr.rtain queerly I edtalora on the road. irrarznacks of Coaster Hubs. Despite the great efforts made to popularise the combination of free wheel and back pedalling brake, familiarly known as a coaster hub, there has always been a shyness displayed towards the contrivance on the part of expert riders, mainly due to the fact that the hub necessarily increased the weight of the driving wheel. A correspondent who has drawn up quite an indictment against the combination hub, 'complains that in addition to the increased first cost and additional weight, the freedom of the free wheel is impeded by the presence of a strong spring, that the braking power is frequently insufficient and nearly always inferior to that of a rim brake, and that there is a difficulty after free- wheeling in picking up an instantaneous drive. It is also contended that, other conditions being equal, a machine fitted with an ordinary clutch will outcoast one fitted with a coaster hub. Possibly the correspondent may have gained his experience on a bad specimen or a poor kind of hub, but we cannot ignore the fact that one hears much less about Coaster Hubs than formerly, and the triumphal progress of the Coaster Hub, which at one time seemed likely to supersede every other kind of free wheel, seems to have suffered a set Itack.

Advice free by letter. , -e. .. , FREE CHURCHMEN AND THE EDUCATION BILL. Action of Free Church Council. The Education Committee of the National Free Church Council held an important meeting on Thursday, at which the following resolutions were passed. It was clebided that a deputation should wait upon Mr. Birrell to emphaise seine of the points dealt with in these resolutions. 1. CLAUSE 7. This Committee notes with the utmost satisfaction the passing of the Seventh Clause with regard to the : T sts for State Teachers by a majority of 191 ; but h Ids that it is necessary in order to make the clause 1 r ally effective, that it should be said. (I) Teachers shall be appointed without referencia to religious creed or denomination or, (2) That the words of Article 15 of the School Management Coda of the late School Board for London should be adopted and used. " The religious opinions of teachers are not to influence their selection, nomination, appointment, or promotion, nor are teachers or candidates for appoint- ment as teachers to be subjected to any questions with reference to their religions belief, or as to the posses- sion or otherwise of certificates which might be taken Iss indicative of belonging to any particular denornina - Lion, nor are inquiries to be made into the religious beliefs of teachers either by the Local Education Authority, local managers, or by any other persons having any connection with the State Education." 11. CLAUSE a. , This Committee deeply regrets that the Sixth Clause was passed by so small a majority ; hut trust that in any event the Government will not fail to maintain , the optional character of attendance at religions instruction as part of the Bill ; since experience shows, (1) That itis the only protection for the child in the rural districts ; and (2) That it is the only method by which the right of parents to be delivered from coercion And intimidation in matters of religion, can be secured. 111. CLAUI,I2 26. That in the opinion of the Education Committee the above Clause should be amended so as to empower at least Urban Education CoUncils of 10,000 population and over to net as! Education Authorities for Elemen- tary Eduaation, suoh authorities to appoint, pay and dismies the teachers. , —5.,..._ COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Nemp's I Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial I Failures recorded in England and Wales during the j -week ending Friday, July ilth, was I:30, viz. :—New ' Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 74; Deeds of Arrangement.registered,76.
